Every start offset of the TypeScript grammar is read after the trivia in front of the first token, as in the JavaScript grammar, so no node starts on whitespace or a comment; before this rule, a parameter list after type parameters, as in `function f<T> (x: T) {}`, and the type parameter of a mapped type after `[` started on trivia. `Keyword` keeps the spelling of a primitive or `unique`/`typeof` keyword in its source span, `Reference` holds an entity name and optional type arguments, `QualifiedName` holds a dotted name, `TypeParameters` records whether its last parameter is followed by a comma, which TSX needs, `TypeParameter` holds its modifiers, name, `Constraint`, and `DefaultType`, and the remaining kinds cover parenthesized, union, intersection, conditional, and tuple types with labeled, optional, and rest elements; `keyof`, `unique`, and `readonly` operators; `infer` with an optional constraint; arrays; indexed access; literal, template literal, `typeof`, and `import()` types with attributes and qualified names; type literals with property, method, call, construct, and index signatures, including optional and definite markers; mapped types with modifiers and `as` remappings; function and constructor types, including the JSDoc-compatible form `function(string): void`; and `this` and `asserts` predicates. Assignability, inference, and compiler-option-dependent rules stay outside this layer.

Class members take their modifiers in any order, and `static` followed by a TypeScript-only modifier, as in `static readonly`, `static override`, or `declare private static readonly`, becomes a modifier node while the member keeps its static flag, which private names and `await` in static initializers depend on. Like in the compiler, `static` may be followed by a line break and the other modifiers may not. Object and mapped types end at their own right brace also inside a template substitution, as in `${value as { a: T }}`. Declarations and statements cover `type` aliases, interfaces with `extends` heritage and members, enums with initializers, namespaces and ambient modules with bodies, the `declare` modifier and ambient contexts, type-only imports, exports, and specifiers, `import x = require("...")` declarations, `export =`, and `export as namespace`, overload signatures as bodyless functions, and decorators. As in the compiler's parser, function declarations and class and object methods may omit their body, but function expressions may not; the `BodylessFunction` node that stands for the missing body spans the return type and the semicolon, or only the semicolon, and is empty where a semicolon is inserted. Expressions and bindings add `as` and `satisfies` at comparison precedence, prefix assertions, `!` in postfix position, instantiation expressions, `this` parameters, annotations with optional and definite markers, modifiers on parameters, object members, and class members including `abstract`, `public`, `private`, `protected`, `override`, `readonly`, and `static`, parameter properties, generic methods and object-literal methods, and annotations on `catch` clauses.

An instantiation expression like `const bound = call<string>` is a `TypeScriptKind::Instantiation` node whose children are the callee and the type arguments. Whether `a < b > c` compares or instantiates follows the pinned compiler. Its closing `>` is re-scanned together with a directly following `>` or `=`, so a list followed directly by `=` is no type argument list: `f<T>= x` and `"s" < b, c >= d` compare, and `f<T>== x` fails as in the compiler. Otherwise `canFollowTypeArgumentsInExpression` decides: a call, a tagged template, or a property access takes the type arguments; a follower that begins with `>`, the single-character `<`, `+`, and `-` keeps the comparison; and otherwise the type arguments are kept when the follower cannot start an expression, is a binary operator, including `=`, `in`, `instanceof`, `as`, and `satisfies`, or follows a line break, while `<=`, `<<`, `!`, and the arithmetic and shift operators instantiate or compare as the compiler decides them. A property access directly on an instantiation expression is a parse error, so `f<T>.x`, `f<T>?.x`, and `new f<T>.x` are rejected while `(f<T>).x`, `f<T>[x]`, and `f<T>(x)` stay valid, which is the rule the compiler reports as error 1477. An expression that starts with `<<` is no assertion or generic arrow function, because the compiler scans `<<` as one shift operator, while `< <T>() => T>value` with a space is an assertion. A heritage clause is not an expression, so its type arguments skip the comparison ambiguity: `class Box<T> extends Namespace.Base<T> implements Contract<T> {}` and `interface I extends First<T>, Second<U> {}` keep them before `{`, `implements`, and `,`.

The redeclaration early errors of section 4.9 are not checked in the TypeScript dialects. TypeScript declares function and method overloads, merges interfaces, namespaces, and enums, and allows ambient redeclaration, so the parser accepts `let repeated = 1; let repeated = 2;` and overload signatures with one implementation, while the JavaScript dialects reject the first. A genuinely conflicting duplicate is a checker diagnostic such as `TS2451`, not a grammar error, and the oracle's parser accepts every case of the comparison battery in section 5, so the parser records and checks no redeclaration in these dialects.

Speculation is restricted to type syntax and function heads, and bodies are parsed only after a head commits. Every consumed token in these dialects increments `ts_work`, which is bounded by the token limit, so a failed attempt cannot parse more than the limit allows, and a rollback restores the cursor, the line-break flag, the token count, the parser context, the `[~In]` flag, the template substitution depth, the conditional-type flag, the cover-grammar marks, and the lengths of the node, child, template-part, comment, bound-name, and expression-only-error lists and of the innermost class's private name references, so a retried reading neither duplicates nor loses a node. `src/parser/speculation.rs` derives the rollback and the record key below from one function that names every parser field in an exhaustive pattern, so a new field does not compile until it is classified (`D-047`): the fields that decide an outcome form the key and are restored; the position bookkeeping, cover marks, and list lengths are restored; the nesting depth, jump targets, declaration scopes, and module state are balanced by the constructs that change them, also on errors; the work counter is deliberately kept; and the lookahead caches hold pure results keyed by offset. Debug builds check at every rollback that the class and declaration scopes are balanced. That check found a namespace body that left its scope pushed and the module's export state replaced when it failed inside a function in a parameter default, which a speculation then discarded; the body now restores both on every path. No tree depended on it, because the other reading parsed the same text and failed again. The four speculations (an arrow function head, the lookahead for a function type, type arguments in an expression, and a constraint after `infer`) run at most once per position and parser state: the parser records each failure and each lookahead answer with the offset, the parser context, the `[~In]` flag, the template substitution depth, and the conditional-type flag, which together determine the outcome, and an arrow head also with its start and whether it is async. The compiler keeps a set of positions that are not arrow functions for the same reason. Without the record, nested constructs that each start a speculation were parsed once inside every failed or discarded reading and again in the next: a parenthesized expression whose content can start a binding pattern, as in `([(a)])`, because heads and the function-type lookahead parse patterns with the cover grammar, and type arguments that contain a parenthesized type, as in `f<([[f<([[a]])>]])>`, which the function-type lookahead parsed as a binding pattern including every nested `f<`. The work at least doubled per level, valid code with 15 levels reached the speculation limit, and a fuzzed 3 KB input took more than ten seconds in an instrumented build (section 7). Speculative work on such nesting now grows at most with the nesting depth times the size of the nested content, and the nesting bound limits the depth. The record lives only during one parse; on the generated TypeScript corpus, it raises the per-file heap peak from a median of 12.5 to 12.9 times the source size (maximum 18.1 to 18.8), while the whole-corpus peak stays at 12.4 times (`D-045`). The dialects are deterministic: the same source, dialect, goal, and limits produce the same tree, comments, errors, and spans.

Assignment targets follow the compiler's parser and checker together: a non-null expression wraps a simple target anywhere (`x! = y`), and `as`, `satisfies`, and angle-bracket assertions wrap one inside parentheses or as a destructuring element (`(x as T) = y`, `[x as T] = y`, `(x as T)++`), while `x as T = y` and `<T>x = y` are syntax errors because an assertion is no left-hand-side expression.

Two deliberate deviations from the pinned compiler remain. Frameshift enforces the ECMAScript early errors that make an invalid assignment, update, `for-in`, `for-of`, or destructuring target a parse error, and it applies that discipline to type syntax and to JavaScript syntax in TypeScript files alike: `f<T> = x`, `f<T> += x`, `++f<T>`, `[f<T>] = x`, `f() = 1`, `(f() as T) = y`, `this = 1`, and `a?.b = 1` are rejected at parse time, where the compiler's parser accepts most of them and its checker reports `TS2364`, `TS2365`, or `TS2356` later. Other ECMAScript early errors that the compiler leaves to its checker, such as a static member named `prototype`, are reported at parse time as well. Second, the compiler rejects a few comparisons whose right operand is an angle-bracket assertion of a tuple or object type, such as `x < <[string]>/re/g` and `x < (<[string]>/re/g)`: it first reads the `<` as type arguments of `x`, recovers from the resulting error, reaches a later `>` before a binary operator, and keeps that reading's error. Frameshift parses these inputs as the comparisons they are. The behavior depends on the compiler's error recovery and is not reproduced; such code did not occur in the real-world sample. Section 5 records how often these differences appear in the oracle comparisons.

## 5. Local validation

From the repository root, with the pinned toolchain installed:

```text
cargo build --workspace --offline --locked
cargo test --workspace --offline --locked
cargo fmt --all -- --check
cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --offline --locked -- -D warnings
```

These commands operate on Frameshift's own code. They do not run any analyzed project's code. Regenerating or checking the Unicode tables requires deliberately downloaded copies of `https://www.unicode.org/Public/18.0.0/ucd/DerivedCoreProperties.txt` and `https://www.unicode.org/Public/18.0.0/ucd/PropertyValueAliases.txt`, kept outside the repository:

```text
cargo run -p frameshift-unicode-gen --offline --locked -- <DerivedCoreProperties.txt> <PropertyValueAliases.txt> crates/frameshift-syntax/src/unicode_tables.rs
cargo run -p frameshift-unicode-gen --offline --locked -- --check <DerivedCoreProperties.txt> <PropertyValueAliases.txt> crates/frameshift-syntax/src/unicode_tables.rs
```

The first command rewrites the tables; `--check` fails if they differ from freshly generated output. The character reference tables work the same way, with deliberately downloaded copies of `https://html.spec.whatwg.org/entities.json` and `https://www.w3.org/TR/xhtml1/DTD/xhtml-lat1.ent`, `xhtml-symbol.ent`, and `xhtml-special.ent`, kept outside the repository:

```text
cargo run -p frameshift-entity-gen --offline --locked -- <entities.json> <xhtml-lat1.ent> <xhtml-symbol.ent> <xhtml-special.ent> crates/frameshift-syntax/src/entity_tables.rs
cargo run -p frameshift-entity-gen --offline --locked -- --check <entities.json> <xhtml-lat1.ent> <xhtml-symbol.ent> <xhtml-special.ent> crates/frameshift-syntax/src/entity_tables.rs
```

Installing the toolchain is a separate development setup operation that may require network access:

```text
rustup toolchain install 1.98.0 --profile minimal --component rustfmt --component clippy
```

The E2 fixtures (section 11) are checked by the test suite and by the driver, which also rewrites the golden files and writes the generated projects to a new directory:

```text
cargo run -p frameshift-e2 --offline --locked -- check
cargo run -p frameshift-e2 --offline --locked -- update
cargo run -p frameshift-e2 --offline --locked -- write --out <new directory>
```

`update` is the only way golden files change. On Windows, the test suite also runs on Linux x86_64 without a Linux toolchain: `rustup target add --toolchain 1.98.0 x86_64-unknown-linux-musl` installs the standard library once, `RUSTFLAGS="-Clinker=rust-lld -Clinker-flavor=ld.lld" cargo test --workspace --offline --locked --target x86_64-unknown-linux-musl --target-dir target/musl --no-run` links static test binaries with the bundled linker, and each binary runs in a WSL distribution with its package directory as the working directory, because the fixture test reads `fixtures/` relative to it.

The suite contains 165 contract tests, 12 printer unit tests, 5 E2 driver unit tests and its fixture test, 4 Unicode generator unit tests, 4 entity generator unit tests, 10 corpus library unit tests, 10 measurement harness unit tests, and 18 fuzzer unit tests. All 229 tests pass on Windows x86_64 and, as static musl binaries, on Linux x86_64 with the pinned toolchain. The Go helper has seven tests of its own (section 9). macOS validation remains outstanding. It includes all 24 permutations of a four-edit example and all 225 pairs of half-open spans in a four-byte source, checking conflicts, stable output, complete source accounting, and output provenance. Lexical tests cover whitespace and comment boundaries, newline evidence, strict-string escapes, permitted and rejected legacy octal escapes, Annex B HTML-like comments in the Script goal and their absence in the Module goal, exact error offsets, limits, and an edit driven by a scanned string span. A generated test exercises all 66,430 strings of length zero through five over a nine-scalar comment alphabet at every offset, checking determinism, comment spellings, and that script trivia extends module trivia. A deterministic robustness test exercises all 7,381 strings of length zero through four over a nine-scalar alphabet, at every scalar boundary and EOF. A reference test compares trivia scanning in both goals with a transcription of the grammar over Unicode scalars, on all 7,240 strings of length zero through three and 20,000 pseudo-random strings of length four through twelve over a nineteen-scalar alphabet, at every scalar boundary and EOF; the alphabet includes U+2027, U+202A, and U+20AC, which share the UTF-8 lead byte of U+2028 and U+2029. Numeric tests cover spelling categories, permitted legacy octal and non-octal decimal literals, exact errors, every byte-limit boundary for representative literals, pinned Unicode identifier-start boundaries, and rewriting from a scanned span. They also exercise all 22,621 strings of length zero through four over a twelve-scalar alphabet at every scalar boundary and EOF. Punctuation tests cover every grammar spelling, all 3,249 ordered pairs of punctuators, all four lexical goals, numeric-dot and optional-chain boundaries, TypeScript type-closing rescans, and shared scanner coordinates. A further generated test exercises all 11,111 strings of length zero through four over a ten-scalar alphabet at every scalar boundary and EOF, in every goal and greater-than mode. Ten template tests cover all four components, tagged/untagged escape handling, original content and delimiter spans, CRLF and Unicode evidence, byte limits, escaped delimiters, parser-selected nested-template boundaries, and a content-span edit. They include all 16,105 strings of length zero through four over an eleven-scalar alphabet at every scalar boundary and EOF, in both positions and modes, with several byte limits. Long code-point escapes check linear progress and overflow safety. Unicode tests classify every Unicode scalar value, compare the counts with the pinned file's totals, check that every start is also a part, and cover ASCII and derived-property edge cases such as Other_ID_Start, Other_ID_Continue, joiners, and Pattern_Syntax letters. Nine identifier tests cover ASCII, Unicode, joiner, and Other_ID names with their boundaries, valid and invalid escapes at exact offsets, private names, all 46 reserved words in plain and escaped spellings, their prefixes, extensions, and capitalized forms, contextual keywords that are not reserved, byte limits, the shared numeric boundary, and an edit driven by an escaped name span. They include all 16,105 strings of length zero through four over an eleven-scalar alphabet at every scalar boundary and EOF. Eight expression-parser tests cover operator precedence and associativity, exact original-source spans, comments, template-part ordering, optional and computed members/calls, regexp-versus-division lexical-goal selection, fail-closed grammar errors, explicit resource limits, and rejection of optional-chain tagged templates through member/call suffixes. Eleven expression-completion tests cover array and object literal forms, holes and trailing commas, assignment associativity and target rules, update expressions, in-place pattern reinterpretation for destructuring and arrow parameters, rest, duplicate-parameter, and `__proto__` rules at exact offsets, `new` argument binding, and the strict-mode-dependent and unsupported forms. Each accepted tree is checked for reachability of every node, nested source-ordered child spans, and an identical arena on a second parse; a generated test applies these checks to all 177,156 strings of length zero through five over an eleven-scalar alphabet. Fifteen regular-expression tests cover lexical extent and errors, every byte-limit boundary, flag validation, Annex B acceptance against Unicode-mode rejection at exact offsets, UTF-16 code-unit class ranges, the named-group reparse, duplicate names across alternatives, modifiers, quantifiers, strict Unicode-mode escapes, exact property names including Unicode 18 scripts, UnicodeSets operators and strings, 200,000-deep nesting, and a body-span edit. They include all 30,941 strings of length zero through four over a thirteen-scalar alphabet at every scalar boundary and EOF, checking that validation agrees with scanning and that errors stay inside the literal. These are bounded generated tests, not coverage-guided fuzzing. None of these tests execute source projects. Before the regular-expression increment was committed, its verdicts were compared once with V8 14.6 in Node.js 26.7.0 (Unicode 17) as a development-time oracle, on 3,968,682 generated fictional patterns in all three modes that formed single literals: the validity verdict agreed in every case, and the capturing group count agreed for all 952,969 accepted patterns. Every pinned property name was also checked. V8 rejects only scripts added in Unicode 18 (Jurchen, Proto_Cuneiform, Seal) and `Hrkt` (`Katakana_Or_Hiragana`), which the specification accepts because PropertyValueAliases.txt lists it. The oracle and its corpus are not part of the repository or of any test. Before the expression-completion increment was committed, its verdicts were compared in the same way with V8 13.6 in Node.js 24.13.0, parsing each fictional expression as a parenthesized strict-mode script with lazy parsing disabled: 1,978,342 exhaustive and random token sequences and 1,420,851 grammar-directed expressions and mutations, of which 303,653 both accepted. Frameshift accepted nothing that V8 rejected, except 74 inputs that hit a V8 deviation: V8 rejects valid destructuring assignments in argument lists after an array literal argument (`f([1], {} = b)`) and accepts a shorthand initializer before one (`f({b = 1}, [] = c)`). Parenthesizing those arguments, which leaves their validity unchanged, made V8 accept all 74. All other rejections of V8-valid input were documented fail-closed errors: unsupported function bodies and methods, and call targets that V8 accepts even in strict mode. Ten script and four class and module tests (`tests/program.rs`, `tests/modules.rs`) cover program spans, comments, and hashbangs, automatic semicolon insertion, loops, `switch`, `try`, labels and jump targets, functions of every form, retroactive strictness, redeclaration rules with the Annex B relaxations, sloppy-mode constructs and their strict errors, `using` placement, class elements and private names, imports, exports, and top-level await, with exact error offsets. A generated test applies the tree invariants to all 22,621 token sequences of length zero through four over a twelve-token alphabet in both goals, and a nesting test drives eight constructs past the depth bound on a 64 MiB thread. Before the complete-grammar increment was committed, `parse_js_program` was compared with V8 13.6 in Node.js 24.13.0 as a development-time oracle, compiling each fictional program with `vm.Script` or `vm.SourceTextModule` with lazy parsing disabled: 673,684 exhaustive token sequences of length zero through four over two twenty-token alphabets in both goals, 480,000 programs from a context-aware generator that produces mostly valid code with deliberate violations and mutations, 120,000 programs from a context-free random generator, and 686 hand-picked edge cases, 1,274,370 in total, of which 193,534 both accepted. Every accepted tree passed the reachability and nesting checks. Frameshift accepted nothing that V8 rejected. It rejected 96 V8-valid inputs, all where V8 deviates from the specification: 93 call expressions as assignment targets in strict code, and 3 sources starting with `-->`. V8 aborted with an internal assertion while formatting the syntax error of 121 further generated programs, which were excluded. Earlier runs of the same comparison found one defect, a binary operator accepted after an assignment whose value is an arrow function with a block body, which is fixed with a regression test, and the two cases in which V8 is stricter than the specification's grammar (section 4.10). Ten JSX tests (`tests/jsx.rs`) cover JSX names, strings, and text with exact error offsets and limit precedence, element, fragment, attribute, and child structure with exact spans, trivia in tags and its absence among children, every JSX error kind, the dialect requirement, function context inside containers, a provenance-preserving rename driven by JSX name spans, five JSX constructs nested past the depth bound on a 64 MiB thread, and the tree invariants on all 22,621 token sequences of length zero through four over a twelve-token alphabet, alone and inside an open element, in both goals. Before the JSX increment was committed, `parse_js_program` with the JSX dialect was compared with `@babel/parser` 8.0.7 in Node.js 26.7.0 as a development-time oracle, comparing acceptance and the kinds and spans of all JSX nodes: 937,729 exhaustive token sequences in four families (whole programs, children, tags, and expression positions) and 440,000 programs from a grammar-directed generator, half of them mutated, each in both goals. Every program that both accepted had identical JSX nodes and spans. All disagreements fell into documented classes: Babel does not validate regular-expression patterns and accepts `<!--` inside module code and `-->` at the start of a script (section 4.10). Hand-picked cases showed two further deviations, where the parser follows the JSX grammar: Babel rejects astral characters in JSX names and accepts a comma expression in a spread child. The oracle, its scripts, and its inputs are not part of the repository. Five character reference tests (`tests/reference.rs`) cover both decoders on hand-picked references at exact spans, the attribute rule, numeric replacements, offset validation, and all 3,257,437 strings of length zero through six over a twelve-scalar alphabet at every offset, checking that every JSX reference is an HTML reference with the same span and, outside the replaced values, the same value. Before they were committed, the generated tables were found identical to the `html.entities` data of Python 3.11, and the decoders were compared on 150,000 generated strings of references and near misses: `decode_jsx_character_reference` with the values that `@babel/parser` 8.0.7 decodes in JSX text and attribute strings, with no difference, and `decode_html_character_reference` in text context on 130,000 of them with Python's `html.unescape`, whose numeric branch drops control code points that HTML keeps and was therefore replaced by the WHATWG rules, with no difference. Twenty-one TypeScript and TSX tests (`tests/typescript.rs`) cover the declaration-file dialect, the regressions found by the `D-043` comparisons below and by the span comparison, nested parenthesized expressions and type arguments under a small token budget, which detects repeated speculation (section 4.15), scope and export balance after failed speculation, the spans of signatures without bodies and of lists after type parameters, and type operators with original spelling, tuples, mapped types, and signatures, type-only imports, exports, and specifiers, `import =` and `export =`, namespaces, ambient declarations, and enums, generics, modifiers, and typed members of classes, typed functions, arrows, binding annotations, and objects, assertions, instantiations, and non-null expressions, the instantiation follower rule and the property-access rule, malformed types, the dialect requirement, and failed speculation under the token limit. Each accepted tree is checked for reachability of every node, nested source-ordered child spans, spans that neither start nor end with whitespace except for programs and JSX text, and an identical arena and comments on a second parse. Before the TypeScript increment was committed, `parse_js_program` was compared with `typescript` 6.0.3 in Node.js 26.7.0 as a development-time oracle on acceptance alone: the engine test sources, a deterministic generated corpus of 120,884 declarations with structured types and single-character mutations, and a focused battery of 131 follower inputs for the instantiation reading (section 4.15). The oracle accepted 56,237 generated inputs and both parsers accepted 55,653; every accepted tree passed the invariants. All 584 disagreements are one class, in which Frameshift rejects an assignment or update whose target is an instantiation expression as an early error and the oracle's parser accepts it and its checker reports `TS2364` or a related error later; in that corpus Frameshift accepted nothing that the oracle's parser rejects. The follower battery agreed on 112 of 131 inputs: 14 differences are that same early-error class, four are the no-space `>` runs in which the oracle re-scans its closing `>` into `>=` and rejects `f<T>== x` while Frameshift reads `f<T> == x`, and one is a `const` declaration without an initializer, where Frameshift reports the ECMAScript early error and the oracle defers it to its checker. The expected verdicts of 62 hand-picked grammar cases and 20 hand-picked redeclaration, overload, and merging cases, which the oracle's parser accepts, were checked against it with no mismatch, and the oracle, its scripts, and its corpora are not part of the repository. Before the `D-043` increment was committed, the directory mode and the generated TypeScript corpus were compared with the same oracles: `typescript` 6.0.3 for TypeScript files and `@babel/parser` 8.0.7 for JavaScript files, on acceptance. On the real-world sample (section 6), the first run found two defects, a `null`, `true`, or `false` literal type at the end of an arrow function's return type (`(): T | null => value`, in five files) and declaration files, which led to the declaration-file dialect. After both fixes, the oracles reject 9 of the 13,292 files and Frameshift rejects the same 9 and one more, and no file that Frameshift accepts is rejected by an oracle (section 6). On the first generated TypeScript corpus, Frameshift rejected 64 files that the compiler accepts and accepted 2 that it rejects, which exposed the five defects fixed in section 4.15: modifiers after `static`, object types in template substitutions, the `>=` re-scan, assertion assignment targets, and `<<` at the start of an expression. The remaining differences were generator artifacts and the recovery deviation of section 4.15, which the generator now avoids by naming the types of its assertions. After the fixes, the compiler and Frameshift both accept all 1,000 files of the default TypeScript corpus. A comparison of further seeds was started and stopped at the project owner's request, so it has no result.

**Span comparison with the pinned compiler (2026-10-13).** The node spans of the TypeScript dialects were compared with `typescript` 6.0.3 in Node.js 26.7.0 as a development-time oracle. A dump of each tree mapped its nodes to 83 shared categories with byte spans: identifiers and literals, calls, members, functions, objects, arrays, templates, binary expressions, declarators, JSX nodes, every type node, signatures, type parameters, declarations, decorators, modifiers, assertions, and instantiations. The compiler's positions, in UTF-16 code units, were converted to UTF-8 byte offsets, and its spans start at `getStart`, after leading trivia. The inputs were the 1,000 files of the default generated TypeScript corpus, 1,000 more generated with seed 1, and the 11,672 `.ts`, `.tsx`, `.mts`, and `.cts` files of the real-world sample (section 6), of which both parsers accepted all but the `className={}` fixture of section 6; 5,466,610 spans agreed. The comparison found one defect: a type predicate in a return type, such as `value is string`, started on the whitespace after the colon, because the lookahead that recognizes a predicate restored the cursor to before that trivia. It is fixed, a regression test checks the predicate spans, and the contract checks of section 6 now reject a node that starts or ends with trivia. Every other difference is representational, in seven classes: `as const` and `<const>` keep `const` as a keyword type, where the compiler has a type reference to an identifier; a class constructor's name is a node only in Frameshift; `import.meta` and `new.target` are one meta-property node without a separate name node; `typeof import("x")` is a query around an import type, where the compiler has one import type node; destructuring assignment targets are patterns (section 4.8), where the compiler has literals; Frameshift wraps declarations with `export`, `default`, `declare`, and decorators, so its declaration nodes start after them, and it keeps `async` and `static` as flags and `get` and `set` in signatures as modifier nodes, where the compiler has modifier tokens; and further node shapes were mapped before the comparison, after which their spans agreed as well: interface bodies are type literal nodes, type arguments of calls, `new`, tagged templates, heritage entries, and JSX element names form instantiation nodes around the callee or name, a template literal type without substitutions is a template type, a JSX member tag name is a JSX member expression, a signed mapped-type modifier such as `-readonly` is one modifier node, a catch parameter has no declarator node, and a class's `extends` clause has no heritage node. The node that stands for the missing body of an overload signature was not compared; the fuzzer later found that it started on trivia when the signature had no return type (section 7). The same comparison on the 1,620 `.js`, `.jsx`, `.mjs`, and `.cjs` files of the sample, which the compiler parses with JSX and Frameshift in the dialects and goals of the directory mode (section 6), compared the 1,610 files that both accept: 128,025 spans agreed, and the only differences were constructor names, meta properties, and modifiers. Of the other ten files, both reject the file with `do` expressions, and Frameshift rejects nine that the compiler's parser accepts: the six compiled MDX bodies with a top-level `return`, the Flow-typed file, and the file with legacy decorators, whose errors the compiler leaves to later phases or reads as TypeScript syntax, and the bundled `yarn` release, which exceeds the default limits. The contract checks of section 6, including the new one for token boundaries, also hold for all 13,281 files of the sample that parse with the default limits. The scripts and dumps are not part of the repository.

macOS validation was outstanding at the end of M0 (`D-049`); current native CI evidence is in section 13. An automated Cargo dependency boundary check now exists (`D-051`). Neither the tests of primitives nor the E2 fixtures demonstrate migration correctness beyond the fixtures.

**Interrupted-run reconciliation (2026-10-12).** The `D-041` run reached its usage limit after successfully pushing `91fb5a9`, `beb2cdc`, `db7c6b5`, and `fadbd57` to `main`. Its JSX parser, character reference decoding, component corpus, and import cleanup were already complete. A subsequent worktree audit found no unintegrated commits. The only uncommitted work was the earlier `D-036` regex draft, whose scanner, flags, limits, tests, and documentation had been superseded by `D-037` and later increments. That draft was preserved in a local Git stash, including its untracked files, and its worktree was fast-forwarded to the integrated implementation. Build, all 152 tests, formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, and both generated-table checks passed again on Windows x86_64. This audit does not complete E1 or E2 or add cross-platform evidence.

**Interrupted-run reconciliation (2026-10-12, TypeScript).** The `D-042` decision was committed as `12dd170` by a Codex run that was interrupted before its parser, tests, and documentation were committed. A follow-up run verified the working tree (build, tests, formatting, and Clippy with warnings denied) and was itself interrupted while updating this documentation. This run completed the documentation, corrected the instantiation follower rule against the pinned compiler, added the property-access rule, and documented the redeclaration rule with its contract test (section 4.15), reran the comparisons above, and integrated the increment under the twelfth one-run exception of `D-010`, with all 164 tests, formatting, and Clippy with warnings denied passing again on Windows x86_64.

## 6. E1 measurement harness

`tools/frameshift-bench` implements `D-040`. Measurements require optimized builds. Printing and contract checks work in debug and release builds:

```text
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --release --offline --locked
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --release --offline --locked -- --seed <n> --files <n> --iterations <n>
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --offline --locked -- --check [--seed <n>] [--files <n>]
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --offline --locked -- --print <index>
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --offline --locked -- --print-component <index>
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --offline --locked -- --print-typescript <index>
cargo run -p frameshift-bench --release --offline --locked -- --dir <path> [--iterations <n>]
```

`--print`, `--print-component`, and `--print-typescript` write one generated file of the mixed, component, or TypeScript corpus to standard output for review. `--dir` measures the files of a directory instead (below). The tool writes no files, starts no processes, and uses no network.

**Contract checks.** `--check` validates every file of all three generated corpora under its declared goal and dialect and default limits. It checks that the program and consumed spans cover all source bytes, every node is reachable exactly once, node and template spans slice the original UTF-8 text and nest in source order, no node other than the program and JSX text starts or ends with whitespace, a line terminator, or a comment, comments are ordered and match their delimiters, a fresh second parse yields identical nodes, child sequences, template parts, comments, and consumed span, and empty edits preserve every byte with complete source provenance. Traversal is iterative and linear in nodes and bytes. Failures stop at the first file and report its corpus, index, goal, dialect, and reason, with exit code 1. Success prints deterministic counts and the existing input fingerprints, without timings or heap measurements. This is an invariant check, not an oracle comparison, coverage-guided fuzzing, or migration verification. The tool still reads no files in generated mode and never executes generated source. `--check`, `--dir`, and the three print modes are mutually exclusive; conflicting modes are usage errors (exit code 2).

**Corpus.** `tools/frameshift-corpus` generates fictional JavaScript with SplitMix64 and integer arithmetic only, so a seed yields byte-identical files on every platform. Every declared name is unique within its file, and each construct appears only where its goal and function context permit it: imports, exports, and top-level await in modules; `require`, `module.exports`, `var`, hashbangs, and `"use strict"` in scripts; functions of every kind, classes with private names, static blocks, and `super`, destructuring, loops, labels, `switch`, `try`, templates, regular expressions, numeric and string literal forms, comments, CRLF line endings, and files without semicolons. Nesting is bounded, so a file exceeds its target size by at most one top-level item (about 1 KB on average). The default corpus has 1,000 files with target sizes piecewise log-uniform from 1 KiB to 32 KiB, about one in ten a script: 10,342,331 bytes, fingerprint `7c71c5e6160d3383` for generator version 1, pinned by a unit test. Before committing, 20,000 generated files (211 MB) were parsed without error, and V8 in Node.js 26.7.0 accepted all of 500 generated files with `node --check`.

**Component corpus.** `Corpus::components` generates JSX modules for the JSX dialect with the same seed, sizes, and generator, from a separately salted random sequence, so the mixed corpus keeps its bytes. About half of the top-level items are function components with destructured props that return multi-line JSX: intrinsic, component, member, and void elements, fragments, string, expression, arrow, spread, boolean, and style attributes, text with character references, expression and comment containers, `map` callbacks, and conditional elements; JSX also appears in other expressions. The default component corpus has 1,000 modules: 10,065,738 bytes, fingerprint `3b5b6cd23fac9fdb`, pinned by a unit test. Every generated component file must parse; before committing, `@babel/parser` 8.0.7 accepted 150 of them (1.4 MB) with identical JSX nodes and spans. The baseline and the throughput increment below measured the JavaScript and JSX corpora; the TypeScript corpus follows.

**Memory budget (`D-045`).** The 20x E1 gate applies to peak heap demand of the whole corpus with every source text and tree retained, not to each file. The harness reports its verdict on that peak; per-file ratios and the largest file peak remain diagnostics. The historical retained figures below do not record the whole-corpus peak separately. An all-empty corpus reports absolute heap and node counts without memory ratios, node density, a node-limit size estimate, or a memory verdict.

**Measurements.** `src/measure.rs` times `SourceText::new` plus `parse_js_program` in each file's dialect, and the parse alone, after one warm-up pass, and reports the minimum and median of the measured passes. Per-file memory is the source size plus the largest heap growth while building the source text and parsing, with the tree alive. A second pass keeps every source text and tree alive at once. A scaling series parses single modules from 1 KiB to 4 MiB with unbounded node and token counts. `src/alloc.rs` is the only unsafe code: a global allocator that forwards to the system allocator and counts requested bytes with relaxed atomics. It does not observe allocator overhead, fragmentation, or the old block of a moving reallocation, so figures are heap demand rather than resident memory.

**Baseline (2026-10-12).** Intel Core i9-14900K desktop, Windows 11, Rust 1.98.0, release build, default corpus, five passes:

| Measurement | Result | E1 budget |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parse throughput, minimum (median) | 11.5 MB/s (9.7 MB/s) | at least 30 MB/s per core: missed |
| Peak heap per file, median (maximum) | 9.9x (14.9x, a 1.7 KB file) | diagnostic only (`D-045`) |
| All source texts and trees retained | 99.8 MB for 10.3 MB of source (9.7x): 3.1 MB indexes, 86.4 MB trees | retained ratio only; peak not recorded |
| Syntax nodes | 1,388,794, 0.134 per byte | |
| Scaling series, 1 KiB to 4 MiB | 6.8 to 13.1 MB/s, peak 6.9x to 11.9x | |

This machine is not the reference hardware, and its hybrid cores make timings noisy, so these figures are evidence rather than a budget verdict. Throughput stays in the same range across three orders of magnitude of file size, so parsing is linear, but it misses the budget by a factor of about 2.6. At the baseline, the parser rescanned trivia and the next token for each peek; the throughput increment below addresses this. Trees hold about 62 bytes per node, although a node is at most 40 bytes, mainly because of vector growth reserve. The default limit of 100,000 nodes corresponds to about 744 KB of this mix, much less than the 16 MiB source limit.

**Allocator regression test.** The local allocator test wraps every allocation and reallocation result in `NonNull` before use. It checks preserved initialized data after both growth and shrinkage, zeroed memory, live-byte accounting, and the peak. Allocation failure stops the test before any memory access. The discovery environment-file test (section 22) uses a fixed assertion diagnostic without printing the fixture path. These test-only safeguards address CodeQL alerts without changing product behavior or suppressing queries.

**Throughput increment (`D-040`).** Counting scanner calls on the default corpus showed 2.2 punctuator scans per consumed token, each comparing all 57 spellings, 4.9 trivia scans, and 0.7 identifier scans per token. Every step below keeps the public API and produces identical trees and errors. Before each step was committed, a development-time comparison parsed the same inputs with the parser before the increment and the new parser, and compared node arenas, child order, comments, consumed spans, and errors byte for byte. The inputs were the 3,000 files of two generated corpora, 40,000 mutations of 1,000 of them (byte ranges deleted, fragments inserted, ranges duplicated), and, from the second step on, all 99,499 sequences of up to three tokens over a 46-token alphabet with line breaks, HTML-like comment spellings, regular-expression and template delimiters, and contextual keywords, plus 200,000 random sequences of four to eight such tokens. Each input was parsed as a script, as a module, and as an expression: 1,027,497 outcomes with 38,954 trees and errors of about 40 kinds. The comparison is not part of the repository. Measurements use the machine and corpus of the baseline:

| Step | Parse throughput, minimum (median) |
| --- | --- |
| Baseline, measured again at the start of the increment | 13.0 MB/s (12.9 MB/s) |
| Punctuator candidates indexed by first byte (section 4.3) | 39.6 MB/s (38.5 MB/s) |
| Lookahead caches for trivia, identifiers, and punctuators (section 4.12) | 53.1 MB/s (51.4 MB/s) |
| Byte-level trivia and ASCII identifier scanning, sorted reserved-word keys (sections 4.1 and 4.6) | 59.9 MB/s (58.9 MB/s) |
| Reused child-list buffers (section 4.12) | 63.4 MB/s (63.2 MB/s) |
| Inlined check for already skipped trivia (section 4.12) | 68.7 MB/s (68.5 MB/s) |

Reusing the hash maps of transient scopes and inlining the cached identifier and punctuator peeks were measured as well. They gave no gain, and the reused maps raised peak memory, so neither was kept.

**Result after the increment (2026-10-12).** Same machine and corpus, ten passes:

| Measurement | Result | E1 budget |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parse throughput, minimum (median) | 68.4 MB/s (66.2 MB/s) | at least 30 MB/s per core: met on this machine |
| Peak heap per file, median (maximum) | 9.9x (15.0x, a 1.7 KB file) | diagnostic only (`D-045`) |
| All source texts and trees retained | 99.8 MB for 10.3 MB of source (9.7x): 3.1 MB indexes, 86.4 MB trees | retained ratio only; peak not recorded |
| Scaling series, 1 KiB to 4 MiB | 58.8 to 83.7 MB/s, peak 6.9x to 11.9x | |

Throughput rose by a factor of 5.3 with identical trees and errors, and it stays in the same range across file sizes. Peak memory is unchanged within 0.1x of the source size. This desktop is faster per core than the reference laptop of section 4.1 of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md), so the result shows a margin of about a factor of two here rather than a verdict on the budget, which is checked on reference hardware. Scanning the corpus with the same public scanners outside the parser takes about two fifths of the parse time; the rest is grammar logic, which still peeks at the next token several times through the cached lookups. A token buffer that scans each token once would be the next structural step if JSX or TypeScript consume the margin.

**After the JSX increment (`D-041`, 2026-10-12).** Same machine, default corpora, ten passes. The mixed corpus contains no JSX; its node counts and peak memory are unchanged, so the dialect parameter and the JSX dispatch cost nothing measurable for JavaScript:

| Measurement | Mixed corpus | Component corpus (JSX) | E1 budget |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Parse throughput, minimum (median) | 69.5 MB/s (66.4 MB/s) | 70.9 MB/s (69.5 MB/s) | at least 30 MB/s per core: met on this machine |
| Peak heap per file, median (maximum) | 9.9x (15.0x, a 1.7 KB file) | 11.1x (17.1x, a 1.4 KB file) | diagnostic only (`D-045`) |
| All source texts and trees retained | 99.8 MB for 10.3 MB (9.7x) | 106.1 MB for 10.1 MB (10.5x) | retained ratio only; peak not recorded |
| Syntax nodes | 1,388,794, 0.134 per byte | 1,484,692, 0.147 per byte | |

JSX parses as fast as JavaScript but produces about ten percent more nodes per byte, so its trees take more memory. The largest per-file peaks belong to the smallest files, whose fixed allocations weigh most.

**TypeScript corpus (`D-043`).** `Corpus::typescript` generates, from a third separately salted sequence, about eleven `.ts` modules, eight `.tsx` component modules, and one declaration file in every twenty files, with the sizes of the other corpora. `tools/frameshift-corpus/src/typescript.rs` adds TypeScript only when this corpus is generated, so the other corpora keep their bytes and fingerprints: annotations on bindings, parameters, and returns; generics with constraints, defaults, and `const` modifiers, including TSX arrows with a trailing comma; interfaces with every member kind; type aliases over keyword, reference, literal, union, intersection, array, tuple, object, function and constructor, conditional and `infer`, mapped, indexed access, `keyof`, `typeof`, and template literal types; enums, namespaces, overloads, type guards, and assertion functions; classes with modifiers, typed and definite fields, parameter properties, accessors, overloaded and abstract members, and decorators; ambient declarations, `declare global` and `declare module`; type-only imports and exports and `import =`; `as`, `as const`, `satisfies`, `!`, angle-bracket assertions, and type arguments in calls and on TSX element names; and typed props and state hooks in components. The default corpus has 1,000 files: 10,397,752 bytes, fingerprint `607a5ff60744ecd6`, pinned by a unit test. Every generated file must parse, and the pinned compiler accepts all of them (section 5).

**Directory mode (`D-043`).** `--dir <path>` walks a directory without following symbolic links, skips `.git` and `node_modules`, and selects files by their last extension, case-sensitively. The tool maps extensions itself; this is not the project layer's classification. `.js` and `.jsx` are parsed as JSX modules, because Next.js compiles JSX in `.js` files, and a file that fails as a module but parses as a script is counted separately; `.mjs` as a JavaScript module and `.cjs` as a script; `.ts`, `.mts`, and `.cts` as TypeScript modules; `.tsx` as a TSX module; and declaration files (`.d.ts`, `.d.mts`, `.d.cts`, and other `.ts` files whose name contains `.d.`, as the compiler decides) in the declaration-file dialect. A file that a node, token, or lexical limit stops is parsed again with those limits raised and counted as parsing beyond the limits. The report lists the parse rate by extension and by top-level directory, every file that is not accepted with its position and error kind, and throughput and memory on the accepted files, with empty files excluded from the per-file ratios.

**Real-world sample (`D-043`, 2026-10-13).** The approved sample of twelve projects at pinned commits, fetched into a cache outside the repository; Windows checks out Git symbolic links as small text files, so the 15 such files were removed from the cache first. Same machine as above, five passes:

| Measurement | Result | E1 criterion |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Files | 13,292 (36.7 MB): 8,098 `.tsx`, 3,427 `.ts`, 1,396 `.js`, 156 `.mjs`, 135 declaration files, 45 `.jsx`, 23 `.cjs`, 12 `.mts` | |
| Parsed | 13,282 (99.92 percent): 13,281 with the default limits, 1 (a 2.2 MB bundled `yarn` release) only with raised limits | at least 99 percent: met |
| Rejected | 10, all explained: 6 compiled MDX function bodies with a top-level `return` (test fixtures of fumadocs), 1 Flow-typed file, 1 file with Babel `do` expressions, 1 file with legacy JavaScript decorators, all rejected by the oracles too; and 1 test fixture named `html--empty-expression.tsx` with `className={}`, which the JSX grammar and Babel reject while the compiler's parser defers it to its checker (`TS17000`) | every failure explained: met |
| Parse throughput, minimum (median), accepted files | 93.5 MB/s (93.0 MB/s) | at least 30 MB/s per core: met on this machine |
| All source texts and trees retained | 298.1 MB for 34.4 MB (8.7x) | retained ratio only; peak not recorded |
| Peak heap per file, median (maximum) | 10.1x (54.6x, a 10-byte file) | diagnostic only (`D-045`) |

The median file and the whole sample stay far within the memory budget, but the 20x ratio does not hold for every tiny file: a tree has fixed allocations of a few hundred bytes, which dominate files of a few dozen bytes, such as a file that only re-exports a name. The project owner resolved this in `D-045`: the 20x budget applies to peak heap demand during whole-corpus retention. Per-file peaks remain diagnostics, including the worst file's absolute heap demand; an all-empty corpus receives no ratio or budget verdict.

**After the TypeScript corpus (`D-043`, 2026-10-13).** Same machine, default corpora, five passes. The mixed and component corpora keep their fingerprints and figures:

| Measurement | TypeScript corpus | E1 budget |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parse throughput, minimum (median) | 49.0 MB/s (48.8 MB/s) | at least 30 MB/s per core: met on this machine |
| Peak heap per file, median (maximum) | 12.5x (18.1x, a 2.6 KB file) | diagnostic only (`D-045`) |
| All source texts and trees retained | 128.7 MB for 10.4 MB (12.4x) | retained ratio only; peak not recorded |
| Syntax nodes | 1,812,415, 0.174 per byte | |

The generated TypeScript is denser than real code, with 0.174 nodes per byte against 0.121 in the real-world sample, because nearly every binding carries a structured type. It parses at about 70 percent of the speed of the JavaScript corpora; the difference was not profiled. On real files, which are mostly TypeScript and TSX, throughput is higher than on any generated corpus.

**Corpus contract and Linux validation (2026-10-13).** On Linux x86_64, Rust 1.98.0, all 175 workspace tests, build, formatting, and Clippy with warnings denied pass. Two release-mode `--check` runs over the default 3,000 files produce byte-identical standard output. All files pass the contracts above, with the same input fingerprints, node counts, and source bytes as the existing Windows measurements:

The whole-corpus peaks meet the `D-045` 20x gate on this machine, and parser throughput exceeds 30 MB/s. A shared virtual machine is not the reference laptop; these are additional platform observations, not reference-hardware evidence or an E1 completion. The check mode also runs in a debug build. Release-mode command checks confirm usage-error exits for conflicting modes, source-only printing, and finite memory reports for empty and 10-byte fictional directory inputs. The real-world sample was not fetched or measured again in this run. Compiler span comparisons, coverage-guided fuzzing, reference-hardware measurements, and the maintainability review remain open; E2 is also incomplete.

**Laptop observation (2026-10-13).** The fuzzing runs of section 7 took place on a Lenovo laptop with an Intel Core Ultra 5 228V (eight cores, four of them low-power efficiency cores), Windows 11 with the balanced power plan, on AC power, while OneDrive synchronized the repository's build directory in the background with about a third of a core. Processes started from the agent session received far less processor time than wall-clock time (section 7), which slowed every timed measurement. Release-mode measurements with the default corpora and three to five passes varied widely between runs, also when bound to one core: parse throughput minimums of 14 to 41 MB/s for the JavaScript corpus, 17 to 33 MB/s for the JSX corpus, and 15 to 26 MB/s for the TypeScript corpus. Whole-corpus peaks stayed at 9.7x, 10.5x, and 12.4x. The interference rules out a budget verdict. The machine belongs to the class of the reference laptop of section 4.1 of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md), however, so the TypeScript dialects may miss 30 MB/s on reference hardware; a controlled measurement there remains open. Alternating measurements before and after the arrow-head fix of section 4.15 in the same session showed no measurable difference.
