Node counts and input fingerprints are unchanged, and the sample still parses at 99.92 percent, with the same eleven files not accepted with the default limits. The scaling series stays between 65 and 83 MB/s from 1 KiB to 4 MiB. The speculation and span fixes of this run left throughput within the range of the earlier measurements. Under `D-047`, these figures decide the E1 throughput and memory criteria; reference-hardware measurements follow before releases in M11.

## 7. Coverage-guided fuzzer

`tools/frameshift-fuzz` implements `D-044`. Fuzzing needs a coverage-instrumented release build in a separate target directory; replaying saved inputs works in any build:

```text
cargo build -p frameshift-fuzz --release --offline --locked --config tools/frameshift-fuzz/instrument.toml --target <host triple> --target-dir target/fuzz
target/fuzz/<host triple>/release/frameshift-fuzz targets
target/fuzz/<host triple>/release/frameshift-fuzz run --target <name> --dir <run directory> [--seconds <n>] [--executions <n>] [--seed <n>] [--max-len <n>] [--timeout <seconds>]
cargo run -p frameshift-fuzz --release --offline --locked -- replay --target <name> <file or directory>...
```

The run directory holds generated data only. A directory below the ignored `target/` or outside the repository keeps it out of commits. The tool writes nothing outside the run directory, starts no process other than its own worker, and uses no network. All inputs derive from fictional seeds.

Use the host triple from `rustc -vV` (for example `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`). An explicit `--target` is required even for native fuzz builds: otherwise Cargo also instruments the engine's M1 host build script, which does not link the fuzz runtime and fails with unresolved coverage symbols. The explicit target keeps build scripts uninstrumented and target crates instrumented. This command correction was verified with the path fuzz increment (`D-054`).

**Instrumentation.** `instrument.toml` passes `-Cpasses=sancov-module` with `-sanitizer-coverage-level=3` and `-sanitizer-coverage-inline-8bit-counters` to the pinned stable rustc, so every basic block of every crate in the binary increments its own 8-bit counter. It also sets one code generation unit per crate, which the feasibility check showed to be required (`D-044`), and enables overflow checks and debug assertions, so silent arithmetic or invariant errors become findings. The standard library is precompiled and not instrumented. On Windows, `tools/frameshift-fuzz-sections` defines the section markers `__start___sancov_cntrs` and `__stop___sancov_cntrs` in `.SCOV$CA` and `.SCOV$CZ`, around the counters in `.SCOV$CM`; ELF and Mach-O linkers define such markers themselves. The instrumented build has about 25,000 counters. In an uninstrumented build or with `RUSTFLAGS` overriding the configuration, no counters are registered, and the worker refuses to run (exit code 2).

**Runtime and features.** Module constructors report the counter section before `main` through `__sanitizer_cov_8bit_counters_init`. `src/runtime.rs` keeps the first nonempty bounds, disables the counters if different bounds arrive, and offers two operations: clear every counter, and copy every counter into a buffer. Both are single `memset` and `memcpy` calls on the static section, from the only thread that runs instrumented code during fuzzing. `src/runtime.rs` and the marker crate are the only unsafe code of the tool. As in libFuzzer, a hit count falls into one of eight buckets (1, 2, 3, 4 to 7, 8 to 15, 16 to 31, 32 to 127, 128 or more), and each pair of counter and bucket is a feature. Merging compares words of eight counters through a bucket table. Counters wrap at 256, which can hide a bucket and weakens only the guidance.

**Worker.** The worker runs on one thread with a 64 MiB stack, far beyond what the parser's nesting bound needs, so a stack overflow points to unbounded recursion. It first executes the target's seeds and the inputs saved in `<run directory>/<target>/corpus/` in file name order, then repeatedly picks two corpus inputs, mutates one, and executes the result until its budget of wall-clock seconds or executions ends. Each execution writes the input to `<target>/current`, clears the counters, runs the target, and copies the counters, so only the code that the input reaches contributes features. An input with a new feature joins the corpus and is saved under its 64-bit FNV-1a fingerprint. The worker prints a status line about once a second and writes the slowest input to `<target>/slowest` at the end. With `--executions`, the same seed, binary, and saved inputs reproduce a run exactly.

**Mutator.** One to eight operations per input: bit flips, random or syntax-byte replacements, insertions of syntax bytes, deletions and duplications of ranges up to 64 bytes, insertions or replacements with a token from the target's dictionary, up to 300 copies of a token for deep nesting, insertions of scalars with lexical roles (no-break space, line and paragraph separators, byte order mark, joiners, identifier and astral characters), and ranges spliced from a second corpus input. Inputs are limited to the target's maximum length and may be malformed or not UTF-8.

**Supervisor.** `run` starts the executable again as a worker process and forwards its status lines, which serve as heartbeats. The run ends with exit code 0 when the budget completes, 1 with a finding, and 2 for usage or environment errors. A worker that reports a violated contract exits with code 1; a panic exits with code 101, and a stack overflow, an abort, or exhausted memory end the process abnormally; a worker without a heartbeat for the timeout (default 10 seconds) is stopped. In each case, the supervisor reads the recorded input and saves it as `<target>/findings/<violation, crash, or hang>-<fingerprint>`, with a description that names the cause, the worker seed, and the replay command. `replay` runs files, or the files of directories in name order, catches panics per input, and reports each outcome.

**Targets.** Every target uses the engine facade with default limits and documented smaller limits where needed. Text parsers reject non-UTF-8 through `SourceText` with the exact `InvalidUtf8` position. The path target checks arbitrary bytes, catalog/unit parsing requires UTF-8, the hash target hashes arbitrary binary input while parsing digests only from valid UTF-8, the collections target interprets arbitrary bytes as keys, slot operations, and charges, the gitignore target reads arbitrary bytes as a `.gitignore` file followed by paths, the json target parses valid UTF-8 as both JSON dialects, the semver target reads valid UTF-8 and each of its lines as ranges, versions, specifiers, and `packageManager` values, and the package target interprets valid UTF-8 that parses as JSON or JSONC as a manifest and as lockfiles.

| Target | Entry points | Contracts | Default maximum |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `program` | `parse_js_program` in all five dialects and both goals | `check::program` of `tools/frameshift-corpus`: complete, reachable, source-ordered trees with valid template and comment spans and nodes that start and end at tokens, identical repeated parses, byte-exact empty edits with full provenance; rejections at a character boundary that repeat | 4,096 bytes |
| `expression` | `parse_js_expression` from offset 0 in all five dialects | `check::expression`: consumption to the end, the tree contracts, and repeatable outcomes | 4,096 bytes |
| `regexp` | `scan_js_regexp` and `validate_js_regexp` at offset 0 | repeatable results; token, body, and flag spans; flags that match their letters; flag and pattern errors inside the literal; lexical errors identical in both functions | 1,024 bytes |
| `lexical` | trivia, string, numeric, punctuator, template, identifier, and JSX scanners in every mode at every character boundary | repeatable results; spans that start at the offset; spellings that match the kind; line terminator flags; comment order and spelling; script trivia extending module trivia; strict tokens equal to sloppy ones and untagged templates equal to tagged ones; error offsets at a boundary at or after the offset | 512 bytes |
| `references` | both decoders, in both HTML contexts, at every character boundary | spans that start at `&`; every JSX reference an HTML reference with the same span and value, except where HTML replaces numeric values or the tables differ (`&lang;`, `&rang;`); attribute references equal to text references, which differ only for unterminated named references before `=` or an ASCII alphanumeric | 1,024 bytes |
| `edits` | `apply_edits` with up to 15 decoded edits and optional small limits | identical outcomes for reversed edit order; `TooManyEdits` first; provenance regions that tile the output and reproduce source and replacement bytes; one record per edit; copied and replaced source ranges that tile the source exactly once; output within its limit; no allocation or internal-invariant errors | 4,096 bytes |
| `astro` | `convert_jsx` on up to 32 JSX elements and fragments of a JSX and a TSX module, with a resolver that accepts every component and hydrates every other one, children bound to `children`, then `print_astro_component` with two-space LF and tab CRLF options | identical repeated conversions and prints; rejections, copied spans, and assumptions anchored in the source; printing of converted markup fails at most at the depth limit; printed provenance that `EmittedText::verify` accepts | 4,096 bytes |
| `paths` | source and output path construction from arbitrary bytes with default and two small explicit limits, plus source/output conversions | agreement with an independent acceptance model; exact byte-limit and UTF-8 errors; repeatability; byte/text constructor agreement; unchanged spelling and components; strengthening and weakening preserve identity | 4,096 bytes |
| `identifiers` | finding/rule/feature grammar and file, route, and config unit constructors with default and two small byte limits | independent grammar and uppercase percent-encoding models; unchanged spelling; repetition; exact serialized sizes and catalog limit precedence | 4,096 bytes |
| `hashes` | `Sha256Digest` parsing, one-shot hashing and `Sha256Hasher` streaming | chunk identity, independent canonical digest grammar, repetition, exact byte limits, raw/serialized round trips and failed updates preserving the prior digest; cryptographic known answers are separate facade tests | 4,096 bytes |
| `collections` | `KeyTable` from byte and byte-string keys with a bound from the first byte, `DenseSlots` insertions and completion, and `Budget` charges | agreement with a sorted model including bound and duplicate precedence, identical results for reversed key order, identity round trips, slot outcomes and counts against a vector model, first missing slot, identical results for reversed insertion, and budget accounting against u128 arithmetic, including overflow | 4,096 bytes |
| `gitignore` | `GitignoreFile::parse` on the bytes before the first double NUL, and `decide` for each following newline-separated path, as a directory when its first byte is odd | identical repeated parses and decisions; pattern counts and decisions equal to an independent memoized recursive interpretation of the pattern bytes; work charges equal to the documented formula; a budget one below the charge fails with the bound and without reaching it | 1,024 bytes |
| `json` | `parse_json` in the JSON and JSONC dialects with default limits, then again at the exact and one-below depth and node limits | acceptance, decoded values, number conversions, shadowed marks, lookups, and the duplicate list equal to an independent recursive-descent model over decoded characters; spans on character boundaries that ascend with identities and nest in their containers; identical repeated parses; JSONC accepting every JSON document unchanged; a canonical rewrite that parses to the same values; inclusive limits | 1,024 bytes |
| `semver` | `VersionRange::parse`, `Version::parse`, `DependencySpec::parse`, and `PackageManagerSpec::parse` on the input and on each of its first 16 lines | identical repeated results; a canonical text that parses to an equal range; `satisfies` equal to an independent text-based precedence model with the prerelease rule on probe versions at and next to every bound; minimums that satisfy and are not above any satisfying probe; precedence equal to the model; versions and `packageManager` values that print as their input; range specifiers equal to the range | 1,024 bytes |
| `package` | `parse_json` in both dialects, then `PackageManifest::from_json` and `Lockfile::from_json` for npm and Bun | identical repeated interpretations; issue and error spans on character boundaries; dependencies equal to their classification and never invalid, with unique names; modeled keys never listed as other members; strictly sorted workspaces and packages; resolutions of declared dependencies that are locked packages; resolution from packages and the out-of-sync check without failure | 4,096 bytes |
| `yaml` | `parse_yaml` with default limits, then the pnpm 9.0 and Yarn Berry lockfile models on accepted streams | repeatable results; node and document spans at character boundaries; graph references to existing nodes; agreement with `parse_json` on the JSON subset; lockfile models sorted with resolvable declarations (sections 27 to 29) | 1,024 bytes |
| `yarn` | `parse_yarn_lockfile` and `Lockfile::from_yarn_classic` | repeatable results; value, member, and key spans; the node bound; key lookups; the classic model's resolution contracts; identical values after writing in Yarn's layout and parsing again (section 29) | 1,024 bytes |
| `workspace` | `select_members` for every `WorkspaceSemantics` over patterns and a directory listing decoded from the input, with the default and a 64-step work bound | repeatable results; independence of the listing order; members only among listed directories with a manifest, with the name and version that Yarn 1 and Bun require; no members after a rejected pattern; equal members under the smaller bound unless it is reported (section 30) | 1,024 bytes |
| `evaluate` | `Evaluator::tree_export` of a detached evaluator for the default, `export =`, and one named export of every tree that parses, with the default and a 16-unit work limit | identical repeated results; results independent of request order with the default budget; values within the depth bound; no unknown part inside a known value | 4,096 bytes |
| `bindings` | `analyze` on every tree that `parse_js_program` accepts in all five dialects and both goals | identical repeated analyses; scopes forming a tree in creation order; references on identifier nodes with their decoded names, resolved to bindings of the same name and an overlapping meaning, sorted by node; every declaration name indexed to its binding | 4,096 bytes |
| `css` | `tokenize_css` and `scan_css_token` in both contexts, `css_token_value`, and `css_dimension_unit` | repeatable tokenization; tokens, comments, and the byte order mark covering the text exactly once with non-empty spans on character boundaries; step-wise scanning equal to tokenization, and scanning at a token's start reproducing it; values of names without escapes or NUL equal to their text; number, percentage, and dimension parts covering their tokens; parse errors in order on character boundaries; coverage with unicode ranges allowed; an inclusive token limit (section 33) | 1,024 bytes |

**Tool validation.** Besides the unit tests (section 5), the supervisor was checked end to end on Windows x86_64 with a temporary build that injected a panic, an infinite loop, unbounded recursion, and a contract violation into one target, triggered by a saved input: each run ended with exit code 1 and saved the triggering input as a crash (exit code 101), a hang, a crash (`0xc00000fd`), or a violation, and `replay` reproduced the panic. The injection was never committed. Per execution, recording the input costs about 8 microseconds and clearing and copying the counters about 3 microseconds on the laptop of the budget run below.

**Limits.** Coverage covers only the crates compiled with the configuration, not the standard library. There is no comparison feedback, so magic values come from the dictionaries. Each worker uses one thread; parallel fuzzing means several runs with different seeds or targets. The budget is wall-clock time, because the standard library has no CPU clock, so a budget run measures the processor time of its workers separately, with the helper of section 9 (`D-046`). Saved corpora are not minimized. The original instrumented builds and supervisor validation ran on Windows x86_64. Linux x86_64 instrumented smoke runs for `identifiers` and `paths` are recorded in section 16; the other targets have no Linux budget evidence. No CI workflow runs the fuzzer yet ([TESTING.md](TESTING.md), section 7). The targets check invariants, repeatability, and independent models, not agreement with an external oracle; the gitignore matcher's oracle comparison with Git and the JSON parser's comparison with `JSON.parse` and Bun are separate development-time checks (sections 23 and 25).

**Budget runs (2026-10-13).** Two runs on the laptop described in section 6, Windows 11, Rust 1.98.0, instrumented release builds, default seed and maximum lengths, all six targets in parallel, each worker with a wall-clock budget:

| Target | Run 1 (`9977734`, 3,600 s): executions, features | Run 2 (`fa7fbae`, 3,720 s): executions, features | Findings |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `program` | 2,512,289, 32,756 | 1,805,027, 31,005 | none |
| `expression` | 2,115,375, 26,418 | 1,170,551, 23,744 (stopped at 3,087 s) | run 2: a hang |
| `regexp` | 29,302,912, 2,901 | 24,036,476, 2,803 | none |
| `lexical` | 2,194,150, 4,356 | 1,643,961, 4,349 | none |
| `references` | 22,027,568, 754 | 18,008,711, 752 | none |
| `edits` | 41,146,098, 895 | 34,963,293, 895 | none |

Neither run found a crash or a contract violation, and every saved corpus input of both runs passed `replay` in an ordinary release build. Both runs exposed exponential speculation in the TypeScript dialects (section 4.15). In run 1, the slowest-input diagnostic did: replayed alone, every slowest input took about as long as starting the process, except an `expression` input with nested parentheses and brackets that the TypeScript dialects rejected with `ResourceLimit` after about 21 milliseconds, while the JavaScript dialect rejected it with a syntax error in 0.4 milliseconds. Fuzzers detect crashes, hangs, and violated contracts, not rejected valid input, so this diagnostic was the only signal. The fix for arrow function heads was then fuzzed in run 2, whose `expression` worker reported a hang after 3,087 seconds: a 2,992-byte input that took about 50 milliseconds per parse in a release build, more than ten seconds for three dialects and two parses each in the instrumented and throttled build, and showed the interplay of type arguments and function-type lookahead. With every speculation recorded, that input parses in 0.4 milliseconds and reports the same syntax error as the JavaScript dialect. Regression tests cover both cases. A differential comparison of all 440,685 program and expression outcomes on the saved `program` and `expression` corpora of both runs, before and after the fixes, found no changed tree: all 530 changed outcomes were former `ResourceLimit` rejections, of which 482 now report a syntax error and 48 reach a limit at a later offset.

These two runs do not satisfy the E1 budget of one CPU-hour per target. Process CPU time, sampled every 15 seconds during run 2, reached only about 970 seconds per worker in 3,720 seconds of wall-clock time. In this environment, processes started from the agent session receive far less processor time than wall-clock time: a single compute-bound process without input or output got about half of one core, and six in parallel shared about two cores while most processors idled. Run 1 was not sampled but ran under the same conditions. A budget run must therefore report the measured CPU time of every worker; the desktop run below does.

**Budget run on the desktop (2026-10-13, `D-046`, `D-047`).** On the development desktop of section 6 (Intel Core i9-14900K, Windows 11, high-performance power plan), every fuzzing run ran under `tools/frameshift-cputime` with a 15-second interval and a wall-clock budget of 4,000 seconds, with the default seeds and maximum lengths and the targets in parallel. Processes received their full processor time there: the final processor time of every worker that ran to its budget, read from the handle that the helper keeps open, was within one percent of the wall-clock time.

| Target | Commit | Worker processor time | Executions | Features | Findings |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `program` | `473f74e` | 3,965 s | 9,453,066 | 38,256 | none |
| `expression` | `473f74e` | 3,965 s | 13,322,554 | 33,787 | none |
| `regexp` | `7c84dc6` | 3,974 s | 227,792,664 | 3,066 | none |
| `lexical` | `7c84dc6` | 3,975 s | 12,714,047 | 4,416 | none |
| `references` | `7c84dc6` | 3,975 s | 185,692,755 | 764 | none |
| `edits` | `7c84dc6` | 3,974 s | 372,186,564 | 969 | none |

The `regexp`, `lexical`, `references`, and `edits` targets exercise code that has not changed since `7c84dc6`, so their results hold for the final commit. The `program` and `expression` targets ran four times while the parser changed. On `7c84dc6`, before the token boundary contract of section 6 existed, both ran for 3,973 CPU seconds without findings (10,123,609 and 16,324,761 executions). On `7f979e2`, with the contract, the `program` target found the node of a bodyless signature that started on trivia after 1,337 CPU seconds, and the `expression` target a parameter list after type parameters that started on trivia after 2,449 CPU seconds. Analyzing the first finding with the pinned compiler also showed that function expressions accepted a missing body. On `91c084e`, which fixed the first two defects, both ran for 946 seconds without findings and were stopped once the second finding showed that the TypeScript grammar read several start offsets before trivia. `473f74e` fixed that class for the whole TypeScript grammar (section 4.15), and the final runs above found nothing. Before they started, all 61,274 saved `program` and `expression` corpus inputs of the earlier runs passed `replay` on the final commit. Regression tests cover every finding.

## 8. E1 maintainability review

**Status: accepted by the project owner on 2026-10-13 (`D-047`), with the follow-up work at the end of this section.** The agent drafted the review at the project owner's request (`D-044`). Section 2.8 of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md) requires a documented review that concludes whether the arena, visitor, and pattern-matching design is maintainable and extensible to further languages. The review assesses the code at commit `c42b749` and the history of the JSX, TypeScript, and declaration-file increments, and separates observed facts from judgment.

**Arena (facts).** A `SyntaxTree` holds a flat vector of `SyntaxNode` values, each a closed `SyntaxNodeKind` and a byte span, at most 40 bytes by a compile-time assertion. `NodeId` is a non-zero 32-bit index, so `Option<NodeId>` stays four bytes. Lists of children are ranges of one shared child vector, and template parts use a second one. Trees store spans rather than token text, and comments are a separate ordered list. The parser builds the arena in source order, reinterprets cover-grammar nodes in place, and rolls back speculation by truncating the vectors. Retained trees take 9.7 to 12.4 times the source size on the generated corpora and 8.7 times on the real-world sample (section 6).

**Arena (assessment).** The layout is simple, compact, and deterministic: there are no pointers, no reference counting, and no lifetimes beyond the borrowed source, and node identity is stable within a tree. Truncation-based rollback is cheap and has been exercised by every TypeScript speculation. The cost is that nodes cannot be removed or reordered after the fact, so later rewriting must stay in the edit layer (`P-003`), which matches the planned architecture.

**Traversal (facts).** `SyntaxTree::child_nodes` is the single traversal primitive: one exhaustive match over all 101 `SyntaxNodeKind` variants, about 270 lines, returning a non-allocating iterator of direct children in source order. Every consumer in the repository (the contract tests, `tools/frameshift-corpus`, the measurement harness, and the fuzzer) traverses through it. There is no visitor trait. TypeScript productions use one generic variant, `SyntaxNodeKind::TypeScript { kind, children }`, with 60 `TypeScriptKind` values whose children are an ordered list.

**Traversal (assessment).** One traversal function suffices for the invariant checks and keeps every new kind visible in one place. Two limits will matter for the semantic layer (M4): consumers of TypeScript nodes must know the position of each child by kind, which the type system does not express, and there are no typed accessors such as "the parameters of this function". A visitor or typed accessors should be added when M4 needs them, not earlier.

**Pattern matching (facts).** Product crates deny Clippy's `wildcard_enum_match_arm`, and every match over `SyntaxNodeKind`, `TypeScriptKind`, `ParseErrorKind`, and the token kinds lists all variants. The parser uses `matches!` 88 times for fail-closed membership tests (section 7.3 of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md)). Twenty match arms in the library crates use `_`: most match characters, bytes, or strings, but four classify an `Option<ReservedWord>` or `Option<Punctuator>` as one of a few unary or binary operators, array delimiters, or `super` forms and otherwise fall back to another grammar path. Each fallback ends in a typed rejection for a token it does not know, but Clippy's lint does not inspect wildcards inside `Option` patterns, so this property rests on review.

**Pattern matching (assessment).** Exhaustive matches made each extension mechanical: the compiler listed every place that a new kind affected. The four `Option` wildcards are a small gap in that guarantee and should become explicit membership tests or carry a comment that explains why the fallback fails closed.

**Extension evidence (facts).** The JSX increment (`91fb5a9`) added 15 node kinds, a scanner module of 142 lines, and a parser module of 358 lines; existing parser modules changed by 9 to 58 lines each, and 663 of its 1,566 inserted lines are tests. The TypeScript increment (`e44a376`) added 60 production kinds behind one new variant, so `ast.rs` changed by 10 lines; the grammar lives in its own module of 1,804 lines, and existing parser modules changed by 8 to 86 lines each. The declaration-file dialect (`9dd3be7`) changed 26 lines of the parser. The two speculation fixes of section 4.15 changed only `parser/mod.rs` and `parser/typescript.rs`. Fuzzing ran about 7.6 million program and expression executions with the tree contracts of section 7 and found no arena or traversal defect.

**Further languages (assessment).** Nothing in the arena is generic yet: the node, kind, and tree types live in `frameshift-syntax` and name JavaScript constructs. The pattern itself (compact nodes with spans in a flat arena, shared child ranges, a closed kind enum, one exhaustive traversal function, and parser state that exists only during one parse) carries over to the planned CSS, JSONC, YAML 1.2, and Astro template parsers. Following architecture driver 7 of [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md), shared arena primitives should be extracted when the second parser needs them. This conclusion is a judgment about the design, not a demonstration by a second implementation.

**Risks found.**

1. *Speculation state in the TypeScript dialects.* `ts_try` saves and restores ten pieces of parser state in addition to its checkpoint of five, and the speculation record keys on five pieces of state. A new parser field must be classified for both, and nothing enforces it. Both exponential defects that fuzzing exposed came from this area. Recommendation: gather the state that speculation saves and that determines its outcome into one structure, so that rollback and the record key derive from one definition, and keep fuzzing the TypeScript dialects.
2. *Module size.* `parser/expression.rs` (2,028 lines) and `parser/typescript.rs` (2,010 lines) are the largest hand-written modules, and `Parser` has 33 fields. Recommendation: split the TypeScript grammar into types, declarations, and expression extensions when it next grows.
3. *Positional TypeScript children.* See the traversal assessment. Recommendation: typed accessors when M4 consumes types.
4. *Error recovery (`D-034`, `D-085`).* The optional recovery API (section 36) discards failed batches rather than exposing partial trees or rolling back failed statement scopes. Nested-list recovery remains a possible extension.
5. *Throughput headroom.* The TypeScript dialects parse at about 70 percent of the speed of JavaScript, and measurements on a reference-class laptop were inconclusive (section 6).

**Conclusion.** The arena, traversal, and pattern-matching design is maintainable: it absorbed JSX, TypeScript, TSX, and declaration files with local changes, and the compiler enforced exhaustiveness at every step. Its weakest part is the speculation state of the TypeScript grammar (risk 1). Extensibility to further languages holds at the level of the design. The project owner accepted the review, so the maintainability criterion of E1 is met.

**Follow-up (2026-10-13, `D-047`).** Risk 1: `src/parser/speculation.rs` now holds the speculation record and rollback. One function names every parser field in an exhaustive pattern without `..`, so a new field does not compile until it is classified, and the record key and the rollback both derive from its result (section 4.15). Its balance checks in debug builds found the namespace body defect described there. The `Option` wildcards: the unary and binary keyword operators, the `super` forms, and the array delimiters are now membership tests whose fallbacks fail closed, with comments that name the rejection, so no wildcard arm over an `Option` of a closed enum remains in the library crates. A development-time comparison of 634,380 parse outcomes before and after both changes found no changed tree or error: three generated corpora of 1,000 files each (seed 1), 26,000 mutations of generated JSX and TypeScript files, and the 13,292 files of the real-world sample, each parsed in all five dialects as a script, a module, and an expression, in a release build with debug assertions. Moving the speculation code shrank `parser/typescript.rs` to 1,896 lines. Risks 2 and 3 remain follow-ups for when the TypeScript grammar next grows and when M4 consumes types; risk 4 is M3 work, and risk 5 is checked on reference hardware in M11 (`D-047`).

## 9. Processor-time helper

`tools/frameshift-cputime` implements `D-046`. It is a Go module outside the Cargo workspace, built and tested with the toolchain that its `go.mod` pins (Go 1.25.5); `gofmt -l` must print nothing:

```text
go -C tools/frameshift-cputime build -trimpath -o ../../target/cputime/ .
go -C tools/frameshift-cputime test ./...
go -C tools/frameshift-cputime vet ./...
gofmt -l tools/frameshift-cputime
target/cputime/frameshift-cputime [--interval <seconds>] [--log <file>] -- <program> [<argument>...]
```

**Behavior.** The helper starts the program with inherited standard input, output, and error. It ignores interrupts itself, so the program decides how to end and the helper measures until it has. Every interval (default 5 seconds) and once more after the program ends, it adopts the program's new descendants from the operating system's process table and reads the user and kernel time of every tracked process. A row whose start time precedes its parent's belongs to an older process with a reused ID and is not adopted. With `--log`, it writes JSON Lines to a new file and refuses an existing one: a `header` record (format `frameshift-cputime`, version 1, platform, interval, and command), one `sample` record per process and sample, and an `end` record with the exit code, whether every sample succeeded, the program's own times from its exit status, and the last values of every process. The summary on standard error lists each process with its processor time and whether the value is final. The exit code is the program's, 128 plus the signal number for a program that a signal ended, or 125 when the helper cannot start or measure the program or a sample failed.

**Platforms.** On Windows, the helper reads the Toolhelp process snapshot and keeps a handle with `PROCESS_QUERY_LIMITED_INFORMATION` and `SYNCHRONIZE` access to every tracked process. The handle keeps the process object alive, so the times of an ended process stay readable and final, and its ID is not reused during the run. On Linux on amd64 and arm64, it reads `/proc/<pid>/stat` in units of `USER_HZ`, which is 100 there. An ended process disappears from `/proc` once its parent has waited for it, so its last sampled value is a lower bound by at most one interval. Other platforms, including macOS, refuse with exit code 125. On both supported platforms, a process that starts and ends within one interval is missed.

**Tests.** Seven tests cover argument parsing, the log and summary layout, the parser of `/proc` stat lines (including names with spaces and parentheses), the adoption of descendants and the rejection of reused IDs, the refusal to overwrite a log, and an end-to-end measurement in which the test binary runs as the measured program, starts a busy child, and exits with code 3. They pass on Windows x86_64 and on Linux x86_64 (a WSL2 kernel 6.6, with a statically linked, cross-compiled test binary). The helper also cross-compiles for Linux ARM64, Windows ARM64, and macOS ARM64. No CI workflow runs these tests yet.

## 10. Astro printer and JSX conversion

`crates/frameshift-emit` turns converted structure into new files (`D-048`). It performs no I/O and selects no rules; the caller decides what to convert and classifies the recorded assumptions.

**Emitted text and provenance.** `SourceId` names one source file by the caller's deterministic index, and an `Anchor` is a span in one source. `OutputBuilder` appends copied source spans and generated text, each with an anchor, under explicit `EmitLimits` (64 MiB of output and a nesting depth of 256 by default); adjacent copies of contiguous source and generated pieces with the same anchor share one region. The resulting `EmittedText` partitions its bytes into regions that are `Copied` (exact source bytes) or `Generated` (text produced for the anchored construct, with no claim of equivalence). `EmittedText::from_rewritten` converts the output of `apply_edits` (section 3), and `verify` checks the contract against the sources: contiguous nonempty regions that cover the text, valid anchors, and copied regions equal to their source bytes.

**Astro component model.** An `AstroComponent` has frontmatter items and template nodes. Frontmatter items are generated imports (default, named, or side-effect, with the specifier printed as an escaped single-quoted string), copied source spans, a generated `interface Props` with `string` members, and a generated `const { ... } = Astro.props;`. Template nodes are elements with an HTML tag or a component name, fragments, text, expressions, and slots. Attributes are valueless, quoted text, an expression, or a spread. Expressions consist of copied source pieces, nested markup, and generated identifiers or `true`. Every name in the model is a validated type: `JsIdentifier` (no escapes, no reserved, strict-mode reserved, `await`, `eval`, or `arguments`), `HtmlTagName` (lowercase ASCII letters and digits with single hyphens), `ComponentName` (an uppercase identifier or a dotted path), and `AttributeName`, so a name can never inject syntax.

**Printer.** `print_astro_component` takes explicit `PrintOptions` (an indentation of 1 to 16 spaces or a tab, and LF or CRLF), so callers pass the project's dominant style. The frontmatter is fenced by `---` and omitted when empty; a blank line separates its imports from the other items, and copied frontmatter that contains a line starting with `---` is rejected, because it would end the frontmatter. Children are placed on separate indented lines only when none of them is text and the element is not `pre`, `textarea`, `listing`, `title`, `script`, or `style`; otherwise, and inside expressions, they are printed inline, so the printer never adds whitespace next to text. Text escapes `&`, `<`, `>`, `{`, `}`, tab, CR, LF, and U+00A0 as references, attribute text escapes `&`, `"`, tab, CR, and LF, and U+0000 is rejected. Void elements print as `<br />` and reject children, empty non-void elements print as `<div></div>`, and components without children print self-closed. The file ends with a line ending.

These rules rest on the behavior of the Astro 7.3.8 compiler, observed on fictional probe pages with the `astro7-preact10` profile: whitespace that contains a line break between markup nodes is dropped as in JSX, but `pre` content is preserved; text without line breaks, character references, and quoted attribute values pass through to HTML unchanged, including `{` and `}` inside quotes; and expressions are rendered with HTML escaping.

**JSX conversion.** `convert_jsx` converts a JSX element or fragment, optionally parenthesized, into template nodes and returns them with the recorded assumptions and the copied spans. It follows React's semantics for the subset of section 9.3 of [MIGRATION_SPEC.md](MIGRATION_SPEC.md):

- Names that start with a lowercase ASCII letter are HTML elements; every other name, including member expressions, goes to the caller's `ElementResolver`, which returns a component, optionally hydrated with `client:load`, or an HTML element that stands for the component (for example `a` for `next/link`). Unresolved components, namespaced names, HTML names with uppercase letters, `svg` and `math` content, children of `script`, `style`, `textarea`, `title`, and `noscript`, and children of void elements fail.
- Text follows Babel's `cleanJSXElementLiteralChild`: lines are split at CR, LF, and CRLF, spaces and tabs are trimmed at inner line edges, empty lines are dropped, and lines are joined with a space. The 253 XHTML references are decoded (`decode_jsx_character_reference`), and the printer re-escapes the value for HTML, so a legacy spelling such as `&copy` stays literal as in React. A tab inside a line, a line or paragraph separator, a control character, a reference to a surrogate, and a reference that decodes to whitespace fail, because the JSX compilers treat them differently.
- On HTML elements, `className`, `htmlFor`, `acceptCharset`, and `httpEquiv` are renamed, 30 further camelCase props with a known HTML name are lowercased, lowercase names including `data-*` and `aria-*` pass unchanged, `key` and the `suppress...Warning` props are removed, and every other name fails, including event handlers, `ref`, `children`, and uncontrolled defaults. A valueless attribute stays valueless for the 24 HTML boolean attributes, becomes `"true"` for `data-*` and `aria-*`, and fails otherwise. Attribute strings are decoded like text and fail if they contain a line break, which compilers normalize differently. `dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: value }}` becomes `set:html={value}` and fails with children or any other value shape. `style` is copied only as an object literal with non-computed keys, non-empty string values, and keys whose CSS names React and Astro derive alike (no `ms` prefix), because React adds `px` to numbers and Astro does not.
- On components, props keep their names, `key` is removed, `ref` and `children` fail, a valueless prop becomes `name={true}`, and markup as a prop value fails (named slots come later). Children of components become their default slot.
- An expression container is copied verbatim between its braces, with the outermost JSX inside it converted in place, so comments in it are kept; an empty container is kept only when it holds a comment. A child expression that is exactly the caller's children binding becomes `<slot />`; any other use or rebinding of that name fails. Spread children fail.
- Every comment inside the converted range must lie in a copied span; a comment in a tag, for example between attributes, fails rather than being dropped.
- Where the result depends on runtime values, the conversion records an `Assumption`: a child expression that could evaluate to `true` (React renders nothing, Astro the text `true`), unless it is a literal, markup, a conditional or `&&` of such, arithmetic, or `map` with an arrow returning such; a non-literal attribute expression on an HTML element (booleans, arrays, and objects serialize differently); a spread on an HTML element; a non-literal prop or a spread of a hydrated component (Astro must serialize it); and a removed `key` expression with possible side effects.

Errors are a closed `ConvertErrorKind` with an anchor and a stable identifier; no partial markup is returned. Walks over expression trees (finding nested markup, checking the children binding, deciding child safety, and removed `key` expressions) use explicit stacks or loops, because the parser builds member, call, and binary chains without nesting, so their depth is bounded only by the node limit. Only markup nesting recurses, up to `max_depth`: in an optimized build, converting and printing 250 nested elements fit in 384 KiB of stack, comparable to the parser (section 4.12).

**Tests.** `tests/astro.rs` checks long member chains on a small stack, exact printed output for HTML attribute rules, text whitespace and references, components, links, islands, and slots, nested markup in expressions, styles, the assumptions, frontmatter printing with tab and CRLF options, generated expression pieces, and 23 fail-closed conversions, and verifies the provenance of every printed result.

## 11. Experiment E2

`tools/frameshift-e2` applies the printer, the JSX conversion, and span edits end to end to three fictional fixtures under `tools/frameshift-e2/fixtures/`, each a minimal Next.js App Router project in `source/` with its golden Astro project in `expected/` and a golden `provenance.txt` (`D-048`).

| Fixture | Source | Output |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `static-page` | Root layout without metadata, a home page with `next/link` links and a JSX comment, and an about page with a module constant, body declarations, comments, references, and links | Layout with the head that Next.js adds, two pages wrapped in the layout, with the comments and declarations in their frontmatter |
| `layout-metadata` | Root layout with `title.default`, `title.template`, and `description` metadata, a global stylesheet, and a server header component; a home page without and an about page with a title | Layout with `Props`, `<title>`, and the description meta tag; the header as an Astro component; pages that pass the resolved title and the inherited description; the stylesheet under `src/styles/` |
| `counter-island` | Root layout, a home page, and a `'use client'` counter component with a comment | The counter kept as a TSX file with only the directive removed, the page rendering it with `client:load`, and the Preact integration with `compat: true` |

**Driver.** The driver recognizes only the constructs of these fixtures and fails closed on everything else: a root layout, static pages, server components under `components/`, client components with a leading `'use client'`, global stylesheets under `app/`, `next/link` with a string `href` and none of its navigation props, `import type` from `next`, relative imports, static metadata with string values, module and body `const` declarations, and a final `return` of JSX. Client components are rewritten by span edits: the directive and the blank lines after it are deleted, and relative import specifiers would be replaced. Layouts, pages, and server components are converted and printed. The head that Next.js renders by default (charset and viewport) and the metadata are inserted into the layout's `html` element, and pages pass the title that Next.js resolves through the layout's template and the nearest description. Imports keep their source order and are rewritten for the conventional layout of `D-024`, the layout import follows them, and comments in the leading gaps of module items and body statements are copied. `package.json` and `astro.config.mjs` are generated from the `astro7-preact10` profile, because E2 does not transform package manifests; `tsconfig.json` is out of scope. Line endings and indentation follow the project's dominant style.

**Checks.** `check` and the fixture test migrate each fixture twice and require identical results, verify the provenance of every output against the sources, require every comment of a converted module to lie in a copied region, and compare every output file and the provenance listing with the golden files byte for byte. The listing, format 1, records the profile, the disposition of every source file (`transformed`, `copied`, `superseded`, or `out-of-scope`) with its outputs, every output region with its origin, and every assumption, with byte offsets (`D-029`).

**Results (2026-10-13).** All three fixtures match their golden files on Windows x86_64 and Linux x86_64. The fixtures record two assumptions, both `child-not-true`, for the about page's `{office}` and `{OPENING_HOURS}`, which are string constants that a rule with scope analysis could prove; the island's literal prop needs none. With the profile installed outside the repository (`D-048`), `astro build` built all three outputs without warnings. The built pages contain exactly the source markup, the head, the title and description where defined, and the stylesheet, and the counter island rendered `Clicked 3 times` and counted to 5 after two clicks in a browser. The outputs and profile are not part of the repository.

**Assessment of `P-003`.** Span-precise trees, the comment list, span edits, and the targeted printer were sufficient; no concrete syntax tree was needed (`D-049`). Two gaps are recorded for later work: comments inside regenerated tags fail closed instead of moving, and the tree has no parent links, so the conversion recognizes attribute containers structurally from the element.

### 11.1 Maintainability review of the E2 code

E2's criteria do not require a review, but the printer crate is product code that M7 builds on, so the agent reviewed it after the experiment, on 2026-10-14, at the project owner's request. Facts and judgment are separated as in section 8.

**Facts.** `frameshift-emit` has 2,928 lines in five modules and `lib.rs`: `jsx.rs` (1,357, of which about 60 are tests), `astro.rs` (662), `output.rs` (446), `html.rs` (231), and `names.rs` (204). The model is plain data with validated names, the printer is one recursive function per node kind, and the conversion is one struct with one method per JSX construct. The E2 driver's `migrate.rs` has 1,843 lines; it is hand-wired experiment code that rules replace in M7. Every match over `SyntaxNodeKind` in both is an exhaustive list, an `if let` chain, or a `matches!` membership test whose fallback rejects, as section 7.3 of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md) allows; no wildcard arm over a closed enum exists. The review found one class of defect: the walks over expression trees recursed once per syntax level, and the parser's nesting limit does not bound tree depth, because member, call, and binary chains are built in loops (section 4.12). A removed `key={a.b.c...}` with 20,000 members overflowed the stack, and a child expression of that length overflowed it at 256 KiB in an optimized build. The walks now use explicit stacks or loops, a regression test converts both cases on a 256 KiB thread in a debug build, and only markup nesting still recurses, bounded by `max_depth`. A new fuzz target, `astro`, converts and prints every JSX element of JSX and TSX modules with a resolver that accepts every component, and checks determinism, anchors, and provenance (section 7). Its first run on the development desktop, 900 seconds of wall-clock time on the final commit, executed 10,047,977 inputs and reached 40,370 features without a finding. Its worker's 64 MiB stack and 4,096-byte inputs cannot expose the recursion defect above, which the regression test covers. The run is a smoke run, not a budget run of one CPU-hour.

**Assessment.** The structure is maintainable and fits the planned layers: the target-independent emitted text is separate from the Astro model, the model from its printer, and React semantics from Astro syntax, so a React-islands target (M18) and the JSON and configuration printers can reuse `output.rs`, and a further source with JSX reuses `jsx.rs`. Three costs will grow with M4 to M7 and deserve attention, none of which requires an architectural change:

1. *Repeated accessors.* The conversion, the E2 driver, and several test files each wrap `SyntaxTree::node`, `children`, and slicing in their own helpers that turn `None` into an error. Typed, fallible accessors on the tree, together with the typed TypeScript children of risk 3 in section 8, should be added before M4 adds the semantic layer's consumers.
2. *Traversal depth.* Every consumer of a syntax tree must traverse iteratively; recursion is safe only over constructs whose depth the parser bounds. This is now a rule in section 4.2 of [ENGINEERING.md](ENGINEERING.md).
3. *Facade size.* The engine facade originally re-exported 132 names flat. The M1 follow-up of 2026-10-14 exposes the `foundation`, `syntax`, `transform`, and `emit` namespaces. Existing M0 root imports remain available, so development tools do not need a simultaneous migration.

**Conclusion.** No fundamental change of the architecture is needed; development can continue on the accepted designs of `P-002` and `P-003`. The three items above are scheduled follow-ups, not blockers.

## 12. M1 CLI shell and build identity

The `frameshift-cli` crate builds the `frameshift` executable. It depends only on the engine facade and the Rust standard library. `src/args.rs` parses the implemented command contract; `src/render.rs` writes help, version, inspection text, and error documents; `src/main.rs` adapts native arguments and standard streams. It reads a project only through the engine's `inspect` pipeline (section 32), never reads configuration or Git, starts no process, and has no migration logic. Commands and options outside the implemented set fail with usage code 2 rather than behaving as placeholders. Help and successful version output go to standard output only. Human-readable errors go to standard error. A failed write or flush exits with 6.

The engine's `version::info()` returns an immutable `VersionInfo`, with the engine's Cargo package version, empty ruleset, knowledge-base and target-profile catalogs, an optionally embedded build commit, and actual compiler identity. `crates/frameshift-engine/build.rs` invokes only Cargo's `RUSTC --version` and embeds its single printable ASCII identity line. The script also validates an optional `FRAMESHIFT_BUILD_COMMIT` as 40 lowercase hexadecimal digits and embeds it. This runs at build time; the engine API retains its no-I/O runtime contract. No Git state, timestamp, host path, or runtime environment affects the result. The package version remains the unreleased `0.0.0`, now inherited from the workspace root. The E2 fixtures are development experiments, so their target profile is not advertised as shipped migration support (`D-050`).

Available commands, from the repository root:

```text
cargo run -p frameshift-cli --offline --locked -- help
cargo run -p frameshift-cli --offline --locked -- help version
cargo run -p frameshift-cli --offline --locked -- version
cargo run -p frameshift-cli --offline --locked -- version --format json
cargo run -p frameshift-cli --offline --locked -- --version
```

After `cargo build --workspace --offline --locked`, the binary is `target/debug/frameshift` (`frameshift.exe` on Windows). The CLI accepts `--format=text` and `--format=json` as well as separated values, before or after `version`. `--version` cannot be combined with any other argument. Help is text only. Unknown commands and options, duplicate options, unexpected operands, and invalid native Unicode arguments return usage errors without echoing user-supplied text. If JSON was explicitly requested, usage errors also write one `frameshift.error` document to standard output. [CLI.md](CLI.md), section 4.9, defines the exact contract; [frameshift.version.schema.json](../schemas/frameshift.version.schema.json) and [frameshift.error.schema.json](../schemas/frameshift.error.schema.json) define the versioned document shapes. The writer escapes JSON strings, preserves UTF-8, uses LF on every platform, and includes no source snippets.

**Tests and golden updates.** Two unit tests cover all JSON control-character escapes and Unicode preservation, and propagation of stream-write and flush failures. Five process-level tests in `crates/frameshift-cli/tests/cli.rs` cover byte-exact output and repeated runs, command aliases and format placement, rejected options and unavailable commands, terminal injection prevention, runtime environment and working-directory independence, and native Unicode encoding failures (unpaired surrogates on Windows, non-UTF-8 bytes on Unix). Golden fixtures contain placeholders only for the engine version and compiler identity, substituted from the build's engine API. Ordinary tests never update them. The explicit update mode is:

```text
cargo test -p frameshift-cli --offline --locked --test cli -- --ignored update_golden_files
```

**Validation (2026-10-14).** On Windows x86_64, the workspace builds offline with the locked dependency graph, all 236 tests pass (the explicit golden updater is the only ignored test), formatting and Clippy with warnings denied pass, and both actual JSON document kinds validate against their schemas with PowerShell `Test-Json`. All Markdown documentation has valid local file links and no em dashes. The CLI adds no external dependency. Native Linux and macOS CLI runs remain outstanding for M1 CI.

At this increment, M1 remained in progress. Foundation findings, stable identifiers, portable paths, hashing, shared limits and deterministic collections, automated boundary checks, CI, cross-platform validation, Release Please, packaging, and branch protection are not implemented by this increment.

## 13. M1 CI and release foundation

Implemented under `D-051` and `D-052`, with the owner's priority, release gate, Go helper, and App choices recorded through the Codex App question interface. Source path preservation is recorded separately in `D-053`; no path types are implemented in this increment.

**Code and configuration.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs formatting, Clippy, native debug/release tests, both Go helpers' tests/vet/gofmt, generated corpus checks, E2 golden checks, and output hash comparison on six platforms. `.github/scripts/check.ps1` checks Conventional Commit subjects and PR titles, all Cargo dependency kinds against explicit layer boundaries, package version agreement, and full Action pins. `.github/scripts/determinism.ps1` writes a sorted SHA-256 list for all E2 output files. `CI required` succeeds only if conventions, every native test, hash comparison, and packaging succeed.

`.github/workflows/package.yml` builds and smoke-tests all six native product targets, using musl and Rust's bundled linker for Linux. `.github/scripts/package.ps1` embeds the checked-out commit, removes the workspace build prefix, checks JSON version identity and help, inspects the committed `workspace-npm` fixture and checks the document kind and members (section 32), packages each binary twice, and requires identical archive hashes. Archive verification and sorted checksum generation are in `tools/frameshift-release`, a Go 1.25.5 standard-library-only development module outside Cargo. It accepts explicit paths and metadata, never executes the product, and exclusively creates new artifact files. tar.gz and ZIP entries use sorted names, normalized modes/ownership, and UTC commit times. Archive inputs are a fixed set of regular files, bounded to 256 MiB each. Every archive is read back to compare bytes and modes before writing. Checksums reject foreign files, symlinks, and empty directories. Third-party table notices are in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`. Under D-066, Frameshift is AGPL-3.0-or-later and LICENSE is a mandatory regular archive input; missing license text fails before any archive is created.

`.github/workflows/release.yml` creates release PRs with the repository-scoped App. Release Please manages `version.txt`, the root workspace version, all Frameshift-only Cargo lock versions, `.release-please-manifest.json`, and the changelog as one product. The `simple` strategy's TOML updaters were tested outside the repository against the actual manifests, producing the expected version updates without changing dependency versions beyond workspace packages. There is no manual bump: all packages remain 0.0.0 until a release PR is deliberately merged. Local CLI goldens represent the build commit through explicit text/JSON placeholders and still validate both known and unknown identities.

**Repository setup (2026-10-14).** The private App `Frameshift Release Automation` (ID 5320049, installation 171472873) is installed only on `eiserv/frameshift`. An authenticated API read-back confirmed selected repository access, contents and pull-request write permissions, and metadata read permission. A short-lived token was minted with that explicit repository scope, its repository list was checked, and the test token was revoked. The private key is an Actions secret; no key or token is committed. `FRAMESHIFT_RELEASE_APP_READY=true` enables release PRs. `FRAMESHIFT_M1_RELEASE_READY=false` prevents tags, releases, and asset upload until M1 acceptance.

**Repository hardening (2026-10-17).** At the owner's request, and verified by API read-back on the still private repository: Actions are limited to GitHub-owned Actions and `googleapis/release-please-action@*`, with full-SHA pinning required for every Action; the default workflow token stays read-only and cannot approve pull requests; no self-hosted runner is registered. A `release` environment admits deployments only from `main` (custom branch policy), and `release.yml` runs its Release Please job in it. The wiki and projects are disabled, and merged head branches are deleted automatically. The App key is now a `release` environment secret and the repository secret has been deleted. A replacement key was authenticated with a temporary installation token scoped only to Frameshift, then the token was revoked. At that checkpoint, `FRAMESHIFT_RELEASE_APP_READY=false` paused release PR automation until the environment-aware workflow reached main. The M1 publication gate remains false. GitHub cannot return a stored secret value. Both unused App keys have been revoked with the owner's confirmation.

## 14. Canonical source and portable output paths

Implemented under `D-053` and `D-054` in `crates/frameshift-core/src/path.rs`, available through `frameshift_engine::foundation`. The owner confirmed strict validation and PR integration after green CI through the Codex App question interface. The existing branch protection remains in force.

**Values and grammar.** `SourcePath` owns a nonempty UTF-8 string relative to the project root. `/` separates nonempty components. A leading slash, an initial ASCII letter followed by `:`, backslashes anywhere, NUL, and components exactly `.` or `..` fail with typed `PathError` values. Nothing is normalized, collapsed, decoded as a URL, case-folded, or repaired. An empty path does not represent the root. Names such as `CON.ts`, `trailing.`, names with colons or control characters, and long components remain source evidence. Input outside this grammar, including a literal POSIX backslash name or native non-UTF-8 name, must be accounted for explicitly by future discovery; these types do not silently omit it.

**Portable output names.** `OutputPath` requires the source grammar and rejects, in every component:

- Unicode controls (C0, DEL, and C1), Windows-reserved punctuation `< > : " | ? *`, and trailing ASCII spaces or dots;
- ASCII case variants of `CON`, `PRN`, `AUX`, `NUL`, `COM1` through `COM9`, `LPT1` through `LPT9`, and the corresponding superscript digits 1, 2, and 3, plus the console aliases `CONIN$` and `CONOUT$`;
- those device stems with extensions, conservatively including spaces before the first extension;
- components over 255 UTF-8 bytes, which also bounds their UTF-16 length to 255 units. This deliberately rejects some Unicode names that NTFS alone permits.

The Windows rules were checked against Microsoft's [file naming documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/fileio/naming-a-file) and [console handle documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/console/console-handles) on 2026-10-14. Source spelling is preserved even after strengthening into an output path. `TryFrom<SourcePath>` validates names without reallocating; `as_source` and `into_source` expose the weaker view without a copy. The existing source value's construction limits remain in effect during conversion.

**Limits, errors, and costs.** `PathLimits` defaults to 32 KiB including separators and 256 components, both inclusive and explicitly replaceable by the caller. Byte size is checked before UTF-8 decoding (`from_bytes`) and structural checks. Within each component, count limits precede syntax checks. Output checks follow successful validation of the entire source structure; within each output component the size bound precedes characters, ending, and device name checks. Errors contain only numeric offsets/counts and fixed explanations, never input content. Validation is iterative, linear in the input, and allocates no storage. Successful constructors reserve storage fallibly and copy once. Derived cloning follows standard Rust allocation behavior. No host path APIs, filesystem, environment, process, or network operations occur.

**Deterministic use and boundaries.** Equality, hashing, and total ordering preserve original UTF-8 bytes. Ordered standard-library collections can use these values as keys. Case variants and NFC/NFD spellings remain distinct values; this is not collision detection. Text returned by `as_str` needs context-appropriate escaping before rendering. These values do not establish filesystem containment or writability. Native conversion, symlink/reparse-point policy, destination-specific length limits, file/directory prefix conflicts, and case/Unicode-normalization collision detection remain discovery, planning, and executor requirements. There is no filesystem executor or new product command.

**Tests.** Eleven public-facade contract tests in `crates/frameshift-engine/tests/paths.rs` cover unchanged source spelling, canonical grammar and byte offsets, invalid UTF-8, exact and zero limits, deep and long paths, portable names and near misses, every device family and reserved control/punctuation class, source/output conversion without copying, byte-wise ordered collection behavior, and errors that do not expose source names or terminal controls.

**Fuzzing.** The eighth target, `paths`, checks arbitrary input bytes against an independent declarative acceptance model with three limit configurations, and verifies deterministic rejection, unchanged spelling, constructor agreement, and source/output conversions (`tools/frameshift-fuzz/src/paths.rs`). A generated test additionally exercises component combinations, every byte value, 255/256/257-byte components, and the default whole-path byte boundary. No product dependency is added.

**Local validation (2026-10-14).** Windows x86_64 passes all 248 Rust workspace tests in debug and release, with the explicit CLI golden updater as the only ignored test. The offline locked workspace build, formatting, Clippy with warnings denied, Cargo boundary/version checks, local documentation links, and the no-em-dash check pass. All 3,000 generated parser corpus files satisfy the E1 contracts, and the three E2 fixtures match their golden output. A 120-second instrumented path smoke run executed 2,542,032 inputs without a finding. This is a wall-clock smoke run, not a measured CPU-hour budget. Integration requires successful native CI for the PR's current revision; local checks and earlier native results in section 13 are not substitutes for that evidence.

After the final lint adjustment, a rebuilt instrumented binary executed another 788,447 path inputs in 30 seconds without a finding, and all 327 saved corpus files from the first run passed ordinary release-mode replay. This supplies final-source evidence in addition to the initial smoke run. Fuzzing with the coverage runtime remains locally validated on Windows x86_64; the ordinary target contract tests also run in native CI.

At completion of the path increment, findings and stable identifiers were still outstanding; sections 15 to 17 record their subsequent implementation, and sections 18 to 21 record hashing, limits, and deterministic collections. Explicit M1 acceptance remains outstanding (`D-063`). No dependency or release gate changes are made by this increment.

## 15. Stable catalog identifiers

Implemented under `D-055` in `crates/frameshift-core/src/identifier.rs`, exposed through `frameshift_engine::foundation`, with no dependencies or runtime I/O.

- `FindingCode::new` accepts exactly `FS[0-9]{4}` and preserves all digits. Reserved ranges and unassigned codes are syntactically representable; membership and meaning belong to the future catalog.
- `RuleId::new` requires `<source>-to-<target>/area.name`, with exactly one `-to-` occurrence (including overlaps), one slash, and two dot-separated rule name segments. `migration_path()` and `name()` borrow those validated parts.
- `FeatureId::new` requires at least two dot-separated name segments. Further segments support nested catalogs.
- Name segments follow `[a-z][a-z0-9]*(?:-[a-z0-9]+)*`. Whitespace, uppercase, Unicode names, empty segments, trailing or repeated hyphens, and escapes are rejected without normalization.
- `IdentifierLimits::max_bytes` defaults to 128 KiB, inclusive. Rule and feature constructors check size before syntax and allocate fallibly after validation. Finding codes have fixed six-byte storage. `IdentifierError` contains only static categories and numeric counts, never input text.
- Private storage preserves valid spelling. Equality, hashing, and total ordering compare those exact bytes, and `as_str()` borrows them. These values do not prove registry membership or advertise a migration capability.

Seven catalog tests in `crates/frameshift-engine/tests/identifiers.rs` cover every four-digit finding code, malformed grammar, size boundaries and precedence, rule parts, ordered collection iteration, and diagnostics without input text. The catalog regression test validates every concrete feature ID in the V1 matrix and an example expansion of each wildcard family, preserving documented two-segment IDs. Focused tests and Clippy pass on Linux x86_64 with Rust 1.98.0 (2026-10-15). Unit IDs follow in section 16, complete finding records and report-local numbering in section 17, and hashing in sections 18 and 19.

## 16. Stable unit identities

Implemented under the owner's encoding decision `D-056` in `crates/frameshift-core/src/unit.rs`, through `frameshift_engine::foundation`, with no dependencies, counters, or runtime I/O. `UnitKind` has the nine kinds in MIGRATION_REPORTING.md section 2.1 and returns their stable spellings through `as_str()`.

`UnitId` constructors distinguish subjects:

- `for_file(kind, &SourcePath, export, limits)` accepts layout, provider, component, route-file, module, or stylesheet. The optional export is nonempty original UTF-8 without NUL. Semantic analysis will check whether it is an actual export; identity does not require a JavaScript identifier spelling.
- `for_route(kind, pattern, limits)` accepts route or endpoint and an original rooted URL pattern. It rejects backslashes, NUL, empty interior components, and `.` or `..` components. `/` and a single trailing slash are preserved. It does not URL-decode bytes or interpret framework parameters.
- `for_config(concern, limits)` uses at least two dot-separated segments with the catalog name grammar of section 15, such as `nextjs.images`.

Serialization is `<kind>:<subject>[#<export>]`. ASCII letters, digits, and `-._~/[]()` remain literal in subject and export. All other original UTF-8 bytes become uppercase `%HH`, including literal percent signs, hashes, colons, spaces, control characters, and non-ASCII text. The file `a#b` produces `component:a%23b`, the export `b` of file `a` produces `component:a#b`, and the file `a%23b` produces `component:a%2523b`. Input is always original text, never a pre-encoded ID. Source spelling, case, and Unicode normalization remain unchanged. Equality, hashing, and ordering compare canonical serialized bytes. This encoding does not replace context-specific escaping in future Markdown or HTML renderers.

`IdentifierLimits::max_bytes` includes the kind, separators, optional export delimiter, and percent expansion. The default is 128 KiB, inclusive, independent of `PathLimits`. Storage is reserved fallibly only after validation and exact sizing. Category checks run first. For file IDs, export shape precedes sizing; for route/config IDs, encoded size precedes subject syntax. Saturating size accounting prevents overflow from accepting a wrapped length; impossible allocation sizes fail reservation. `IdentifierError` adds static categories for wrong subjects, malformed route patterns, invalid concerns, and invalid exports. Errors never copy source text.

Twelve facade tests in `crates/frameshift-engine/tests/identifiers.rs` cover catalog and unit contracts, every documented unit example, all subject categories, delimiter and percent-sequence collisions, Unicode normalization and case distinction, control encoding, exact limits, canonical ordering, and rejected patterns and exports. A generated adversarial set verifies that different subject/export pairs produce distinct keys. The ninth fuzz target, `identifiers` (`tools/frameshift-fuzz/src/identifiers.rs`), checks grammar and serialization with independent models and three byte limits, as well as repeated outcomes and source spelling. The public constructors take UTF-8; malformed bytes are never decoded lossily by the fuzz target. Its generated test covers fragment combinations, every byte value, and all 10,000 finding-code spellings.

At this increment, M1 remained in progress. Section 17 adds complete finding records and report-local ordering, and sections 18 to 21 add hashing, limits, and deterministic collections. Discovery, actual unit construction from project facts, parsing serialized IDs in document readers, and project collision checks remain future subsystem work. No migration or interactive CLI command is added.

Formatting, workspace/all-target Clippy with warnings denied, the PowerShell crate-boundary/version/Action-pin checker, all 3,000 generated E1 corpus contracts, and E2 goldens pass. Both Go helpers' tests, vet, and formatting pass with Go 1.25.5. The instrumented release build registers 30,892 counters. A 45-second `identifiers` smoke run completes 236,841 executions without findings; a 15-second `paths` smoke run completes 446,714 without findings. These are wall-clock smoke budgets, not the processor-hour criterion of the historical E1 closure. Fuzz artifacts stay outside the repository. Cross-platform evidence for this increment belongs to its native CI run, not these local results.

All 260 workspace tests pass in debug and release after the correction, as do focused fuzzer tests, formatting, and workspace/all-target Clippy with warnings denied.
